Sourcery with Molly O'Shea 2 episode(s) · 62 insights

Anthropic Hits $2 Trillion in Historic IPO | Altimeter's Four Seasons of AI52 min37 insights2026-11-20
▶ Watch on YouTube
Altimeter's Apoorv argues AI is in inning one of a super cycle ($860B big-tech capex, agentic usage surging) and calls Anthropic's S-1 a rare profitable, hyper capital-efficient hyper-grower fairly priced at 11-14x forward revenue for a $1.5-2T IPO. He frames 'four seasons' (Anthropic, OpenAI, SpaceX, Google) plus evergreen open source, highlighting SpaceX's spot-priced compute rental, Google Cloud's 103% growth, a looming US data center capacity shortfall, and cyber as the next big AI market.
AI capex scale and the early-innings super cycle · 5From chatbots to agents: usage power laws · 4Anthropic S-1 and IPO valuation · 7Compute capacity, data center constraints and SpaceX's spot-rental model · 8OpenAI and Google: frontier rivals and the cloud inflection · 3Open source as the evergreen season and inference clouds · 5Growth benchmarks, value accrual and cyber as an emerging AI market · 5
AI capex scale and the early-innings super cycle 5
Big tech AI capex is about $860 billion this year, has more than tripled in two years, now rivals US defense spending, and already exceeds prior booms (railroads, oil and gas, telco, shale) as a percent of GDP.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highbig techAI capexrailroadsshale
So what: Capex magnitude is historic; the key debate is whether it keeps growing past railroad-era peaks, which drives semis and data center demand.
“It's about $860 billion this year. Um over tripled in the last two years.” ▶ 2:53
Big tech capex is flowing dollar-for-dollar into large semiconductor revenue, so the AI economy is currently semis-heavy: models and apps generate roughly $140-150B of revenue atop a much larger semis base, versus the cloud software economy where ~$600B of app revenue (Salesforce, Adobe, ServiceNow) sits on ~$300B infra and ~$80B semis.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highSalesforceAdobeService Nowsemiconductors
So what: AI value today is inverted toward chips; watch for the stack to rebalance toward apps as in prior cycles.
Prior super cycles like mobile accrued value first to semiconductors, then to infrastructure and devices, and only from the third inning onward to software and apps; the AI economy is following the same sequence.
framework🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediummobilesemiconductorssoftware
So what: Positions for a rotation of value toward the application/model layer in later innings.
While the overall AI economy has grown about 6x in the last two to three years, the apps and models layer has grown about 100x over the same period.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAI appsmodels
So what: Top-of-stack growth is far outpacing the stack, supporting the thesis of value migrating upward.
“So the economy has grown 6x the apps and models has grown over about 100x.” ▶ 5:32
Altimeter believes AI adoption is still accelerating rather than plateauing and that the AI super cycle is at its start, not its peak.
thesis🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAltimeterAI adoption
So what: Guest's base case argues against bubble-top positioning on AI infrastructure and leaders.
From chatbots to agents: usage power laws 4
AI has phase-shifted from a chatbot era (about 80% knowledge retrieval use cases, starting with ChatGPT in November 2022) to an agentic era beginning around end of last year, where about 80% of use is coding and agentic automation, including by non-technical sales, marketing, support and investment teams.
framework🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highChatGPTagentic AIcoding agents
So what: Agentic workloads consume far more tokens; favors compute and frontier-model providers with strong agent/coding products.
Candidate modalities for a third AI phase include proactive agents, very long-running agents and agent swarms, and large models performing 'surgery' on smaller models.
prediction🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: exploratoryproactive agentsagent swarms
So what: Early signals of where next token-demand step-ups and startup opportunities may come from.
OpenAI data shows its top 10% of customers grew usage 17x over the last year and a half versus 2x for the median customer, so the gap between frontier firms and median firms is widening.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Diligence portfolio companies on whether they are frontier AI adopters; laggards risk falling behind.
“top 10% of their customers have grown their usage 17 x while their median customer has grown their usage 2 x.” ▶ 9:08
At the individual level, the top 10% of users account for about two-thirds of all tokens, a power law the guest says creates a large catch-up opportunity as broader adoption grows.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumtokenspower users
So what: Remaining population catch-up implies substantial latent token demand growth.
“top 10% of users make up two thirds of all tokens.” ▶ 10:13
Anthropic S-1 and IPO valuation 7
Fortune 500 customers spending over $100,000 on Anthropic say it is less than 0.1% of their total expenses, which the guest sees as proof adoption is in inning one with room to go to 1% and drive another 10x.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAnthropicFortune 500
So what: Low wallet share at large customers suggests long runway for enterprise AI spend.
“Fortune 500 customers that spend more than $100,000 on Anthropic say that it is less than 0.1% of the total expenses.” ▶ 0:15
Per the S-1 as described, Anthropic has grown 10x per year for three years, did about $17B of revenue in Q3, has about 12,000 customers paying over $100K and about 1,900 paying over $1M, with no customer concentration and a reported 900% revenue retention figure.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAnthropicS1
So what: Called the fastest scale-up in history; diversified base reduces concentration risk for IPO buyers.
“It's the fastest scale up in history. It's and it's not even close.” ▶ 12:49
Anthropic burned less than $10 billion of cash to reach roughly a $70 billion revenue run rate, which the guest calls an unprecedented level of capital efficiency for a deeply profitable business.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAnthropic
So what: Counters the 'labs burn endless cash' bear case; capital efficiency supports IPO valuation.
“Less than 10 billion of cash.” ▶ 13:48
Anthropic's compute cost to serve a dollar of revenue has fallen from 88 cents to 15 cents, yielding roughly 85% margins, and its monetization of compute has risen from about $16 a watt to about $60 a watt.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAnthropiccompute
So what: Inference unit economics are improving sharply; margin structure resembles software, not a capital-intensive utility.
“serving a dollar of revenue has gone down from 88 cents of compute [clears throat] to 15 cents.” ▶ 14:16
Anthropic orchestrates heterogeneous compute across TPUs, Trainium and Nvidia GPUs, and the guest believes this hard-won compute efficiency gives it moats.
causal🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumAnthropicTPUsTraniumsNvidia
So what: Multi-vendor compute strategy benefits Google TPU, Amazon Trainium and Nvidia simultaneously and reduces single-supplier risk.
Anthropic's rumored IPO range of about $1.5-2 trillion equals roughly 11x (low end) to 14x (high end) forward GAAP revenue, based on the guest's estimate of about $140-150B of GAAP revenue next year; he calls this not a heroic multiple and expects a very successful IPO despite possible short-term choppiness.
prediction🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAnthropicIPO
So what: Guest frames the IPO as reasonably priced versus comps; a key test for private AI valuations.
“Like it's not a heroic multiple. It's completely reasonable based on current market comps.” ▶ 49:42
The guest views Anthropic and OpenAI as the 'private Mag Seven', likely to be leading companies for the next decade and more, and says Anthropic is far out in the lead in a 'game of emperors'.
thesis🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAnthropicOpenAIMag Seven
So what: Positioning for long-duration ownership of frontier labs once public.
“this is the private mag seven.” ▶ 50:01
Compute capacity, data center constraints and SpaceX's spot-rental model 8
The US will end this year with about 35-40 gigawatts of compute capacity online; following recent data center results/regulation, the guest estimates a 10-15 gigawatt shortfall versus the 40-50 gigawatts of additions forecast for next year.
prediction🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumdata centersgigawattsUnited States
So what: Capacity shortfall raises compute scarcity and pricing; watch permitting/regulatory news as a driver.
“maybe there's a shortfall of 10 15 gigawatts to what we had forecasted for next year.” ▶ 20:37
Holders of the largest pools of existing compute (hyperscalers such as Amazon, Microsoft, Google, and SpaceX) benefit from a capacity shortfall, while neoclouds and startups further back in line for capacity face an existential threat.
causal🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highAmazonMicrosoftGoogleSpaceXneo clouds
So what: Favor incumbents with installed capacity; be cautious on neoclouds dependent on new builds.
“I think for them this is existential, right?” ▶ 22:14
More than half of data center build-out capital goes to IT equipment (chips, memory), with the remainder to getting the facility itself built.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumdata centerschipsmemory
So what: IT equipment vendors capture the majority of each data center dollar.
SpaceX reports about a $60B revenue run rate, roughly half from its AI unit, about a third from Starlink and the rest from launch, and its AI business added over 20% relative market share in Q3.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highSpaceXStarlink
So what: SpaceX is now primarily an AI infrastructure story, not a space story.
SpaceX rents compute at spot prices of roughly $25-50 a watt (its CFO said the higher end), about double the $10-20 a watt of 1-3 year leases from neoclouds like Corby (likely CoreWeave) or Nebius, and Anthropic can pay because it monetizes compute at $50-100 a watt.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highSpaceXNebiusAnthropicneo clouds
So what: Spot compute pricing far above lease rates signals extreme scarcity; supports infra margins while frontier models out-monetize.
“Short-term compute from from SpaceX that is is roughly double that. It's probably like $25 to $50 a watt.” ▶ 34:10
By renting at spot prices SpaceX recoups its data center capex in less than a year, versus the two to three years typical for neoclouds.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumSpaceXneo clouds
So what: Payback asymmetry is a competitive edge, but depends on short-term pricing persisting.
SpaceX's guidance of half a trillion to a trillion dollars of revenue next year rests on going from about 3 GW of compute at year-end to about 13 GW next year at $50+/watt, with risks around sustaining short-term pricing and whether Anthropic renews (likened to Airbnb versus a yearly lease).
prediction🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: lowSpaceXAnthropic
So what: Revenue guidance would exceed frontier labs but is highly sensitive to spot price durability and customer concentration.
“It's like, you know, renting your home on a long yearly lease versus renting a home on Airbnb” ▶ 37:07
Sam Altman and Elon Musk were the two players who made the compute bet early and correctly, pressing to procure compute ahead of revenue (e.g., OpenAI's trillion dollars of commitments against $13B of GAAP revenue questioned on BG2).
contrarian🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumSamElonOpenAIBG2
So what: Early compute procurement is now a strategic advantage amid scarcity, vindicating once-criticized commitments.
OpenAI and Google: frontier rivals and the cloud inflection 3
OpenAI has shown resilience since a 'code red' around year-end: ChatGPT exceeds 1.2 billion users, new models and Codex/ChatGPT work products performed strongly in Q3, and it ended September at about a $50B revenue run rate, above guidance, after hiring sales leaders including former Wiz and SpaceX CROs.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highOpenAIChatGPTCodexWiz
So what: OpenAI remains neck-and-neck with Anthropic; enterprise sales build-out is a key watch item.
“OpenAI has shown incredible resilience and antifragility” ▶ 23:18
Google is a tale of two cities: search at about $250B run rate growing 12% is slowing, while Google Cloud (including TPUs) is about $120B run rate growing 103% year over year, roughly 8x faster than search.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highGoogleTPUGoogle Cloud
So what: Cloud/TPU growth may be underappreciated relative to search deceleration; a potential re-rating driver.
“This is a business that is 120 billion of revenue run rate growing at 103 % year over year.” ▶ 38:04
TPU demand is broadening beyond Google: Anthropic is a customer, OpenAI was rumored to consider TPUs, Oracle was the first outside cloud provider, and Anthropic has a commitment with Broadcom to lease TPUs.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumTPUAnthropicOpenAIOracleBroadcom
So what: TPU ecosystem growth benefits Google and Broadcom and is a competitive vector against Nvidia.
Open source as the evergreen season and inference clouds 5
Altimeter's mental model is that 20-30% of all AI spend will go to open source, with the cash register ringing at the inference layer (players like Fireworks, Modal and Base 10), which it expects to become large businesses.
framework🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumopen sourceFireworksModalBase 10
So what: Open-source inference platforms are a distinct investable layer capturing gross margin.
The best open-source frontier model today is roughly where the closed frontier was six months ago.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumopen source models
So what: Six-month lag bounds closed-model pricing power for non-frontier workloads.
“the best open the frontier open source model today is where the close frontier was 6 months ago.” ▶ 28:28
Open-source adoption is driven by AI-native companies (e.g., Cursor, Decagon, Harvey) wanting control, cost and sovereignty by fine-tuning smaller models on their own data, with enterprises such as banks and healthcare starting to follow; about 35 companies run 99% of all inference per Groq's CEO Jonathan Ross.
causal🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumCursorDecagonHarveyJonathan Rossopen source
So what: Inference demand is concentrated; enterprise open-source adoption is the next wave to watch.
“You own that intelligence as opposed to renting that intelligence.” ▶ 26:16
OpenAI partnered with Base 10 so large OpenAI customers can burn down their OpenAI commits on open-source models, which the guest reads as OpenAI moving from lab to platform.
data_point🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumOpenAIBase 10
So what: Validates open-source inference demand and Base 10's positioning; signals labs embracing platform economics.
The open-source inference cloud market will likely mirror AWS/GCP/Azure with several great businesses and no dominant player, and leaders like Base 10 are moving up the stack with platform tools (routing, sandboxes) toward looking like hyperscalers.
prediction🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumAWSGCPAzureBase 10
So what: Expect fragmented but profitable market; platform tooling launches are a sign of hyperscaler ambitions.
Growth benchmarks, value accrual and cyber as an emerging AI market 5
Everyone in the stack (chips, data centers, clouds, models) will win, but so far it has been a 'game of emperors' where the biggest balance sheets won; the guest hopes more value accrues to models, apps and startups as compute efficiency improves and models get cheaper.
thesis🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumchipsdata centerscloudsmodels
So what: Near-term favor scale players; longer-term watch for value shift to application layer.
“so far it has been a game of emperors and you've seen the biggest players with the biggest balance sheet win” ▶ 40:44
New best-in-class growth benchmarks for AI companies are roughly 10x at single-digit billions of revenue (Cursor rumored 1 to 10, Base 10 10x for two years), 3x at double-digit billions (OpenAI ~20-21B to an estimated 70-80B), and about 2x at $100B+ scale (Google Cloud), replacing software's triple-triple-double-double.
framework🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumCursorOpenAIBase 10Google
So what: Use scale-adjusted growth bars to separate momentum plays from true leaders.
Altimeter views great founders as the leading indicator of startup success and numbers as lagging, citing Cursor's Michael Truell and its new investment in Bob McGrew's company Arda.
anecdote🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumAltimeterMichael TruelBob McGrewArdaPalantir
So what: Signals Altimeter backing of Bob McGrew's new venture.
Cyber is the biggest emerging AI-consuming market because AI gives attackers potent tools to probe every vulnerability (e.g., a one-click Google Chrome takeover exploit found by an AI security team), which will force defensive AI token spend to rise sharply.
thesis🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: highcyberGoogle Chrome
So what: Expect rising security AI spend; continuous AI-driven testing replaces annual pen tests.
“You got to match fire with fire.” ▶ 46:59
The guest expects frontier labs (OpenAI and Anthropic) rather than standalone cyber startups to capture most AI cyber demand because frontier capability matters so much, with harness companies layered on top.
contrarian🗣 Apoorv (Altimeter)conviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropiccyber
So what: Be skeptical of cyber AI startups without durable edge over frontier-model capabilities.
"It's So Over?" Emmett Shear, Brendan Foody, Byron Deeter & Nico Rosberg on Tech's Future49 min25 insights2026-11-21
▶ Watch on YouTube
Molly O'Shea's hallway interviews at the Cerebral Valley AI Summit, recorded during a week when Jensen Huang reportedly left Nvidia's CEO role, the NASDAQ fell and Anthropic's IPO priced at about $1.4T. Byron Deeter shares AI 100 data ($3.6T total, $36B mean valuation, 20% of companies under two years old). Other guests discuss inference as an early exponential, Emmett Shear's critique of 'pretend-to-be-nice' alignment training, verification as the new bottleneck, orbital robotics economics and why more jobs may exist in 10 years.
Jensen exit, Anthropic IPO and the AI sentiment shock · 4AI 100: private-market scale and concentration · 5Inference, compute and shifting bottlenecks · 5Alignment, trust and verification · 2Physical AI, robotics and space · 4Jobs, expertise and the founder environment · 3Security and resilience risks · 2
Jensen exit, Anthropic IPO and the AI sentiment shock 4
Following news that Jensen Huang is no longer Nvidia's CEO, the NASDAQ dropped and Anthropic's IPO priced at about $1.4 trillion rather than the hoped-for $2 trillion, with a robotics founder expecting further downside.
data_point🗣 Ethan Barhaus (Aaris founder)conviction: mediumNvidiaAnthropicNASDAQJensen Huang
So what: Lab IPO pricing is the key sentiment barometer; a markdown from $2T to $1.4T signals tighter risk appetite for AI private marks.
“You see Anthropic come out at 1.4 instead of two. And you know, that's going to drop even worse.” ▶ 22:41
The lesson of the Nvidia leadership shock is that companies cannot depend on a single ecosystem (Nvidia chips, models and Isaac-style tooling) and need supplier diversification.
thesis🗣 Ethan Barhaus (Aaris founder)conviction: highNvidiaIsaac
So what: Favors alternative accelerator vendors and multi-vendor strategies; single-vendor dependence becomes a diligence red flag.
“Everybody can't be reliant on Nvidia and only Nvidia's chips and their models” ▶ 23:25
Despite the market drop, the Anthropic IPO and Jensen's resignation, a founder argues AI is headed for a soft landing because Anthropic's S-1 shows sticky revenue and strong growth and alternatives to Nvidia are emerging, predicting a big dip followed by a quick recovery with consolidation.
contrarian🗣 Ethan Barhaus (Aaris founder)conviction: mediumAnthropicNvidia
So what: Contrarian buy-the-dip framing for AI; the S-1 revenue retention data is the fundamental check on that view.
“I think there's a big dip, but I think it comes back up very, very quickly.” ▶ 29:11
A key risk from Jensen Huang's exit is weaker open-source AI support, since Nvidia was one of the biggest US backers of open source, spending about $30 billion in the ecosystem in the first half of the year, and it is unclear whether a successor will continue.
data_point🗣 Nico Rosbergconviction: mediumNvidiaJensen Huangopen source
So what: Watch whether Nvidia's new leadership keeps funding open-source models; a pullback would hurt open-model developers and startups built on them.
“one of the greatest supporter especially in America of open source um was Jensen” ▶ 30:43
AI 100: private-market scale and concentration 5
The average company on Bessemer's AI 100 list is valued well over $1 billion and has raised over $1 billion, with a mean valuation of $36 billion, meaning dozens of billion-dollar companies did not make the cut.
data_point🗣 Byron Deeter (Bessemer)conviction: highBessemerAI 100
So what: Shows how concentrated and expensive the top of private AI has become; entry prices for leaders are extremely high.
“the average valuation on that list is 36 billion.” ▶ 14:10
About 20% of companies on Bessemer's AI 100 list are less than two years old, reaching billions in value in under 24 months, and the average listed company is growing about 500%.
data_point🗣 Byron Deeter (Bessemer)conviction: highBessemerAI 100
So what: Unprecedented speed of value creation; investors must underwrite very young companies at large scale.
“20% of the list is less than 2 years old.” ▶ 14:21
The AI 100 is worth $3.6 trillion in its debut year, versus the Cloud 100 reaching $1 trillion over a decade, which Byron Deeter frames as a super cycle combining the internet and cloud waves because the industry is now monetizing compute.
thesis🗣 Byron Deeter (Bessemer)conviction: highBessemerCloud 100AI 100
So what: Frames AI as an order-of-magnitude larger market than cloud, supporting continued large private allocations.
“This list in its debut year is 3.6 trillion.” ▶ 14:54
There is no pure-play AI software company trading in public markets, so essentially all AI innovation value is being captured privately.
thesis🗣 Byron Deeter (Bessemer)conviction: highAI 100public markets
So what: Public investors lack direct exposure, so lab and AI-app IPOs could see heavy demand; private access is the edge.
“There is not a pure play software AI company to trade on in the public markets.” ▶ 15:47
Contrary to expectations, cybersecurity and physical AI/robotics make up only a small share of the AI 100, while Bessemer is especially excited about life sciences and healthcare and sees robotics as a next horizon.
data_point🗣 Byron Deeter (Bessemer)conviction: mediumBessemercybersecurityroboticslife scienceshealthcare
So what: AI cyber and robotics are still early-stage categories, which may leave room before they reach scale.
“We were expecting to see a lot more cyber emerging on this list already.” ▶ 16:06
Inference, compute and shifting bottlenecks 5
AI inference is extremely early: the AWS CEO told Alex Heath that today's inference volume is not even 1% of what he expects, and Heath argues people are failing to think exponentially about inference just as they missed foundation models.
thesis🗣 Alex Heath (Sources / Sound Ventures)conviction: highAWSinferenceAnthropic
So what: Position for inference demand growth (inference providers, chips, serving infra) while staying skeptical that inference providers become new hyperscalers.
“it's like not even 1% of what he thinks it will be.” ▶ 18:19
Nico Rosberg is invested in Etched and calls inference an enormous, almost infinite market, saying Etched is at the frontier of delivering inference infrastructure.
thesis🗣 Nico Rosbergconviction: mediumEtchedinference
So what: Inference-specific silicon like Etched may gain from Nvidia uncertainty and from inference demand.
“inference is going to be an enormous kind of infinite market” ▶ 33:11
In the data center buildout the bottleneck has shifted from GPUs to optical interconnect and turbines, which James of Felicis uses as an analogy for how bottlenecks move.
data_point🗣 James Deweiler (Felicis)conviction: mediumdata centersGPUsoptical interconnectturbines
So what: Look at optical interconnect and power generation (turbines) suppliers as the current constraints in AI infrastructure.
“the bottleneck is not GPUs, it's optical interconnect or turbines.” ▶ 41:20
Data centers in space have gone from unthinkable three years ago to a hyperscaler talking point, and a founder predicts big announcements from major AI labs and expects companies like StarCloud to raise hundreds of millions to billions.
prediction🗣 Ethan Barhaus (Aaris founder)conviction: mediumStarCloudhyperscalersdata centers in space
So what: Orbital compute is an emerging theme to watch for lab partnerships and large raises.
“You're going to see some really big announcements from some of the big AI labs about it.” ▶ 26:36
Emmett Shear argues that vocabulary, reasoning and programming have all been commoditized with diminishing returns to more logic and more facts, so the remaining frontier of value is social modeling, which he calls 'friends'.
contrarian🗣 Emmett Shearconviction: mediumreasoning modelsprogramming
So what: If reasoning is commoditized, model-level differentiation erodes; value shifts to relational and social capabilities.
“we've commoditized reasoning, right?” ▶ 1:08
Alignment, trust and verification 2
Emmett Shear warns that training AIs in simulations to maximize scores for socially positive behavior while being watched is about the most dangerous thing you can do for alignment, because it trains them to pretend to care, which is the sociopath's skill.
contrarian🗣 Emmett Shearconviction: highAI alignmentreinforcement learning
So what: A critique of current RL and alignment practice; labs that rely on behavior-under-observation training face latent safety risk.
“the most dangerous thing you can do is train an I AI to uh to perform certain socially positive behaviors so long as it's being watched.” ▶ 4:58
AI has cut the cost of work to near zero, but most businesses do not see the value because the bottleneck moved to verification; Neom says it built tech that collapses verification cost to zero via runtime alignment and governance.
thesis🗣 Alex Garden (Neom)conviction: highNeomverificationruntime alignment
So what: Verification and governance tooling is a key unlock for enterprise AI ROI and an investable category.
“we took a bottleneck that used to exist where work was and we moved it forward to verification.” ▶ 36:33
Physical AI, robotics and space 4
Software moats are weaker than thought because software is now cheap, so durable moats come from hardware and real physical deployments.
thesis🗣 Ethan Barhaus (Aaris founder)conviction: highsoftwarehardwarephysical AI
So what: Favor companies with physical deployments and hardware over pure software, which is easier to replicate.
“Software that moat is not there as much as we thought.” ▶ 23:37
Astronauts on the space station cost about $130,000 an hour to keep alive, and four of them spend 14 days of each 60-day cycle moving about 3.5 tons of cargo, the task Aaris's orbital robots, handed to NASA in January, are built to take over.
data_point🗣 Ethan Barhaus (Aaris founder)conviction: highAarisNASAInternational Space Station
So what: Quantifies the economic case for orbital robotics; space maintenance and logistics is a real addressable market.
“astronauts cost about $130,000 an hour to keep alive.” ▶ 24:17
Nico Rosberg says it is still unclear which robotics approach will win despite huge investment, and general-purpose robots that can handle unprepared random tasks are 'miles away'.
thesis🗣 Nico Rosbergconviction: mediumrobotics
So what: Robotics winners are not yet determined; caution on paying up for general-purpose robotics bets.
“that's miles away.” ▶ 35:14
Felicis highlights Enigma, which is rethinking both robotics foundation models and the interface between humans, agents and robots, and Sulcoa, a rare earth metals company it counts among its favorites.
anecdote🗣 James Deweiler (Felicis)conviction: mediumFelicisEnigmaSulcoarare earth metals
So what: Names to track for next-wave robotics and critical-minerals supply.
“Sulcoa makes rare earth metals.” ▶ 44:02
Jobs, expertise and the founder environment 3
Brendan Foody of Mercor predicts there will be more jobs in 10 years than today, because people underestimate the elasticity of demand for labor and many physical and inherently human tasks will take a very long time to automate.
contrarian🗣 Brendan Foody (Mercor)conviction: highMerkorelabor market
So what: Counter to AI job-loss narratives; supports human-in-the-loop labor marketplaces like Mercor.
“I think there's going to be more jobs in 10 years than there are today.” ▶ 47:07
James of Felicis argues human expertise is losing value as a source of purpose because models can generate it, and the premium shifts to imagination, out-of-distribution thinking, agency, trustworthiness and local knowledge.
thesis🗣 James Deweiler (Felicis)conviction: mediumFelicisexpertiseagency
So what: Framework for which human skills and businesses keep pricing power: trust-based, local and agency-driven ones.
“expertise as something that a human possesses is possibly on the way out.” ▶ 40:46
Alex Heath, now a VC at Sound Ventures, says the strongest early founder signal is the ability to recruit a killer team early, combined with an 'overly audacious' energy that is rare and identified mostly by pattern matching.
framework🗣 Alex Heath (Sources / Sound Ventures)conviction: mediumSound Ventures
So what: Use early recruiting quality as a leading indicator in seed diligence.
“the ability to recruit early is a huge signal for me.” ▶ 20:40
Security and resilience risks 2
Nico Rosberg sees cybersecurity as a major opportunity given current breaches in which attackers are obtaining GitHub tokens (source code access) and credit card data.
thesis🗣 Nico Rosbergconviction: mediumcybersecurityGitHub
So what: Rising AI-era breaches support demand for cyber startups, even though cyber is underrepresented on the AI 100.
“they're managing to get like GitHub tokens” ▶ 35:32
Aiden Guo of Attention predicts cell towers are likely to go down for a few days within six months and advises buying local hardware capable of running a small open-source model.
prediction🗣 Aiden Guo (Attention)conviction: lowopen source modelslocal hardware
So what: A tail-risk view supporting on-device and edge AI hardware demand; low credibility and speculative.
“everyone should try and buy some form of local hardware” ▶ 45:23

278: Paul Kedrosky, Managing Partner, SK Ventures55 min28 insights2026-11-20

Paul Kedrosky (SK Ventures, MIT) argues the AI capex boom is a uniquely fragile bubble because it combines all four historical bubble pillars (technology, real estate, credit, government), with Nvidia acting as the 'central bank of AI' and neo-clouds and Oracle as debt-laden recipients of fragility. He sees a de facto tightening cycle (Texas go-slow policy, Nvidia leadership change), overestimated TAM from hyper-deflating tokens and a software-engineer early-adopter cohort, a Koo-style rate-insensitive deleveraging ahead, and a tail risk of white-collar labor itself being repriced.
Four-pillar bubble framework: tech, real estate, credit, government · 4Private-market froth and a Koo-style balance-sheet deleveraging · 2Overbuild as feature: tech works, investors pay · 3Token hyperdeflation and the TAM extrapolation error · 6Sources and destinations of fragility: Oracle, neo-clouds, Nvidia as central bank · 5De facto AI tightening cycle: Texas, midterms, Nvidia leadership change · 3Misleading indicators and the true tail risk: repricing white-collar labor · 5
Four-pillar bubble framework: tech, real estate, credit, government 4
Kedrosky argues the biggest manias in Western economic history (South Sea Bubble, railroads, canals, rural electrification, fiber, the GFC) each rested on one or more of four pillars: a technology story, loose credit, real estate, and active government encouragement.
framework🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highSouth Sea BubblerailroadsfiberGFC
So what: Use the four-pillar checklist to gauge how systemic a boom is; more pillars present means more fragility.
The current AI build-out is unusual because it combines all four pillars at comparable strength: AI as the technology, loose credit, data centers treated as commercial real estate yield instruments, and a US government that sees AI as an existential race with China.
thesis🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highAIdata centersChinaCRE
So what: Kedrosky sees this as an 'almost irrational bubble'; size systemic tail hedges accordingly rather than treating AI risk as a single-sector bet.
“Data centers become like apartment buildings full of GPUs” ▶ 0:29
Executive orders over the past two and a half years, including EPA changes allowing broader sulfur dioxide emissions for behind-the-meter natural gas turbines that avoid grid interconnection, have created a cumulative policy tailwind accelerating the AI build-out.
causal🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highEPAnatural gas turbinesbehind the meter power
So what: Government support acts like a put under AI capex; watch for policy reversals as a key unwind trigger.
Participants in each silo (powered-land real estate investors, GPU technology investors, lenders) each view their own activity as rational, but the confluence of their discrete activities across the economy is what creates systemic fragility.
framework🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highpowered landGPUs
So what: Fragility is emergent and invisible from within any one asset class; cross-asset monitoring is required.
Private-market froth and a Koo-style balance-sheet deleveraging 2
The combined private-market valuations of SpaceX, Anthropic and OpenAI are roughly equivalent in nominal terms to the peak valuation of the Nasdaq at the top of the dot-com bubble.
data_point🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highSpaceXAnthropicOpenAINasdaq
So what: Equity froth has migrated to private markets; IPOs like Anthropic's transfer that valuation risk to public investors.
“SpaceX, Anthropic, and OpenAI in nominal terms, the valuations in the private markets of those three firms are roughly equivalent to the peak valuation of the Nasdaq at the peak of the dot-com bubble.” ▶ 14:27
The best analog for the aftermath of the AI capex boom is Richard Koo's balance-sheet recession (Japan after its 1980s-90s real estate boom): hyperscalers that entered with pristine balance sheets are leveraging up and will face a prolonged deleveraging largely insensitive to falling interest rates.
prediction🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highRichard KoohyperscalersJapan
So what: Don't count on rate cuts to revive capex after the peak; position for a long, rate-insensitive capex downturn.
“what we will see on the other side of this is a prolonged deleveraging that will be largely insensitive to interest rate declines because they are deleveraging” ▶ 15:50
Overbuild as feature: tech works, investors pay 3
Believing AI is a transformative technology and believing its financing is a bubble are not contradictory; electrification, autos and railroads were consequential technologies that still produced financial crashes such as 1929 and repeated Erie Railroad collapses.
contrarian🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highAIErie Railroadelectrification
So what: Separate the technology call from the investment-return call; the tech can win while capital is destroyed.
“this is a false dichotomy” ▶ 17:20
Like canal, railroad, electricity, office-automation and fiber build-outs, AI capex shows a growing share of non-residential fixed investment, a rising contribution to GDP growth, and leakage into other markets, including AI fundraising pressuring Treasury markets at the margin in late summer.
data_point🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: mediumTreasury marketsnon-residential fixed investmentGDP
So what: Watch AI debt issuance as a source of rates-market pressure and crowding out.
Unlike prior episodes, today's participants explicitly justify overspending by citing Carlota Perez's thesis that overbuild cycles always work out; using historical description as ex-ante justification adds a feedback loop that accelerates both the build-out and its eventual failures.
contrarian🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highCarlota PerezTechnology Revolutions and Financial Capital
So what: When 'it always works out' becomes the alibi, discount management capex rationales more heavily.
“People weren't telling themselves when they built the railroads that overbuilding railroads always works out, right?” ▶ 21:27
Token hyperdeflation and the TAM extrapolation error 6
Kedrosky describes AI tokens as the first hyper-deflationary industrial commodity, deflating by some measures about 13-fold year over year, which implies token volume must double roughly every 95 days just for revenue to stand still.
data_point🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: hightokensAI
So what: Track token volume growth versus price declines; any slowdown in volume below a ~95-day doubling implies shrinking revenue.
“That implies that roughly every 95 days the volume of tokens must double just to stand still.” ▶ 23:11
The industry implicitly assumes infinite price elasticity of demand for tokens, extrapolating three or four years of data, but demand is bounded, so interlocking growth and price-collapse exponentials make overbuild and fragility more severe.
contrarian🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: hightokens
So what: Be skeptical of Jevons-style demand arguments used to justify capex; elasticity will flatten.
“I do not want 100 cars if the price of cars drops to zero.” ▶ 24:31
Ramp's spending data shows venture-backed companies are by far the largest per-employee spenders on AI, by something like 99 to 1, meaning AI demand depends heavily on unprofitable, VC-funded firms.
data_point🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: mediumRampventure-backed companies
So what: AI revenue is exposed to VC funding conditions; a venture downturn would hit AI vendor revenue directly.
Early leaks from Anthropic's S-1 showed that about a year ago Meta was roughly 18% of Anthropic's revenue, illustrating heavy customer concentration in the software industry.
data_point🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: mediumAnthropicMeta
So what: Scrutinize customer concentration in frontier-lab filings; loss of one big buyer is material.
AI adoption is concentrated in software engineers because code provides a ground-truth signal (it works or it doesn't) that enables autonomous, token-heavy iteration, whereas memos, emails and slide decks lack that signal and degrade with unsupervised iterations.
causal🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highsoftware engineersgradient descentlarge language models
So what: Token-intensive agentic usage may not generalize to the broader white-collar market; model demand curves accordingly.
“Can I let a model loose on a memo and have it get better over the next 12 hours? It will get uniformly worse.” ▶ 31:54
Naive extrapolation from this unusual early-adopter cohort leads to a huge overestimation of AI's total addressable market, violating the venture-capital tenet of never extrapolating your eventual market from your earliest buyers.
thesis🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highAITAM
So what: Haircut AI TAM and revenue forecasts built on coding-tool adoption curves.
Sources and destinations of fragility: Oracle, neo-clouds, Nvidia as central bank 5
Oracle is a 'destination of fragility': it is using prodigious debt to escape a mature, declining database business via AI, teetering near another ratings downgrade, with its CDS and bond spreads trading accordingly.
thesis🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highOracleORCLOpenAI
So what: Oracle CDS and spreads are a key stress gauge; a downgrade could be an early contagion event.
Many neo-clouds pivoted from unprofitable crypto mining to AI compute, using vast amounts of debt to build out GPU farms, making them recipients of fragility.
causal🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highneo-cloudsCoreweavecrypto
So what: Neo-cloud credit is among the most vulnerable links; watch their CDS and refinancing.
Kedrosky calls Nvidia the 'central bank of AI', running a quasi-fractional reserve system through unsecured lending and repayment schedules stretched into the future without cash exchange, effectively creating a currency akin to a shadow banking system.
framework🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highNvidiaNVDA
So what: Nvidia's vendor financing is the system's liquidity source; any tightening there propagates to its customers.
“I often call Nvidia the central bank of AI” ▶ 36:20
Frontier AI labs sit awkwardly in the middle of the system, neither true cloud providers nor able to stand without them, and Kedrosky does not hold much hope for them over time.
contrarian🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: mediumfrontier AI companiesAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Be cautious on frontier-lab equity, including IPOs, given structural dependence on cloud providers.
Nvidia's summer plan to backstop GPU prices with a roughly $500 billion facility reportedly involving Blackstone and Goldman was effectively an attempted currency peg that the market saw as insufficiently backed and that never fully materialized.
framework🗣 Dean Curnutt (host) / Paul Kedroskyconviction: mediumNvidiaBlackstoneGoldmanGPUs
So what: GPUs function as currency in AI; attempts to peg their value signal stress and invite a 'break the peg' moment.
“This was an attempt at a currency peg, right?” ▶ 43:54
De facto AI tightening cycle: Texas, midterms, Nvidia leadership change 3
By AI data center build-out, the largest 'sovereign' is the US excluding Texas, second is China, and third is Texas, whose governor-elect has announced a go-slow policy on data centers after the midterms.
data_point🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highTexasChinadata centers
So what: State-level policy shifts in Texas are a major swing factor for data center capex and power demand.
“The third largest in the world is Texas.” ▶ 40:28
Kedrosky argues the post-midterm period is a clear tightening moment for AI that the market initially misread as positive before selling off; the bull argument that fewer data centers protects margins is faith-based since inference capacity can be built in China or elsewhere.
contrarian🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highdata centersChina
So what: Treat restrictive data center policy as negative for AI capex names, not margin-protective.
“you no longer have an empirical argument, you have a theological argument” ▶ 42:04
With Nvidia moving to an interim CEO, its earnings call signaled more caution on lending to neo-clouds, which account for something on the order of 60% of token sales, meaning neo-clouds have entered a credit tightening cycle.
prediction🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highNvidianeo-cloudsCoreweave
So what: Reduced Nvidia vendor financing raises neo-cloud funding costs and default risk; CoreWeave CDS is a tell.
“Neo Clouds just found out that they're they just entered a tightening cycle.” ▶ 43:07
Misleading indicators and the true tail risk: repricing white-collar labor 5
GPU rental price indices (e.g., from Bloomberg) are close to useless as AI health indicators because they average across very dispersed providers and conflate tight-supply spot buyers with hyperscaler long-term contract pricing, a Simpson's-paradox problem.
contrarian🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highGPU rental pricesBloombergSimpson's paradoxhyperscalers
So what: Don't take flat or rising GPU rental prices (as cited by Jensen Huang) as evidence the AI cycle is healthy.
Nvidia has repeatedly protected margins through engineered scarcity: first in premium gaming after losing the mainstream graphics market to consoles, then in crypto (gross margins around 55-60%), and again with generative AI after crypto fell off in 2022.
causal🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: mediumNvidiaATI TechnologiescryptoPlayStation
So what: Current GPU pricing reflects deliberate scarcity, so margins may compress once scarcity can't be maintained.
Kedrosky's 'out there' tail risk: AI providers justify capex by targeting a material share of global white-collar labor (around $5 trillion of revenue by 2031-32), but capturing a slice of a commodity reprices the whole market, shrinking the white-collar labor pool itself.
prediction🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: exploratorywhite collar laborAI capex
So what: The bigger systemic risk may be labor income and tax-base erosion, not CDS or token prices.
“You don't reprice the corner of a commodity. You reprice the entire thing.” ▶ 51:04
Kedrosky calls the rapid expansion of AI-enabled cyberattacks 'the hackening': about 8% of all critical software vulnerabilities ever found were found in the first three weeks of October this year, and 50% since January.
data_point🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: highcybersecuritysoftware vulnerabilities
So what: Expect elevated systemic cyber incidents (airlines, banks, gas supply); cyber defense spend and insurance repricing follow.
“50% of all the critical software vulnerabilities in installed software were found since January of this year.” ▶ 26:02
Kedrosky frames early-stage venture as buying long-dated call options in illiquid markets, and only invests when a company has a plausible mechanism to go 'explosively right' and the option is cheap enough.
framework🗣 Paul Kedroskyconviction: mediumventure capitalSK Ventures
So what: Apply convexity discipline: seek cheap long-dated optionality with an identifiable explosive upside path.
“inexpensive long-dated call options on things that could go explosively right” ▶ 54:20
279: Vineer Bhansali, Founder and CIO, LongTail Alpha46 min25 insights2026-11-21
▶ Watch on YouTube
Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha) tells Dean Curnutt that AI-driven speculative fervor is at a peak, with a VIX above 30 reflecting crowded dispersion and pod-shop unwinds against hidden E-mini illiquidity. His bimodality hypothesis explains 80-100% long-dated vol on Micron, SpaceX and the newly listed Anthropic as rational binary-outcome pricing, and he favors 2-year Treasuries as a carry-plus-optionality hedge against a Fed pivot in a crisis.

AI-bubble backdrop: 1999-2000 rhymes and private-market valuations in public markets 3

thesis🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highCiscoNvidiaAI

“I think right now the speculative fervor is at absolute peak.” ▶ 3:53
Private markets have migrated into public markets: investors pay private-market valuations for low-float listings such as SpaceX and Anthropic without control or voting power.
thesis🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highSpaceXAnthropic
So what: Low-float mega-IPOs carry private-market pricing and squeeze dynamics; expect distorted price discovery and option surfaces.
“the private markets have found their way into the public markets” ▶ 3:18
Benchmark pressure forces institutional managers into high-flying index heavyweights; some investors who fought the AI rally capitulated and bought exposure outright or via options for fear of lagging.
anecdote🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha); Dean Curnutt (host)conviction: mediumAIinstitutional managers
So what: Capitulation buying by former skeptics is a classic late-stage signal; watch for FOMO-driven call demand.

VIX shock, dispersion unwind and hidden illiquidity 6
Low implied volatility does not preclude extreme moves: the narrower the distribution, the fatter the tails, so 5-10 sigma events at a 15 vol are unsurprising, especially with the S&P 30-40% and the QQQ 70-80% driven in risk terms by seven or eight stocks.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highS&P 500QQQmegacap stocks
So what: Do not anchor risk on normal-distribution sigmas in a concentrated index; tail hedges are underpriced when vol is compressed.
“the narrower you make the distribution, the fatter the tails get” ▶ 5:53
Single-name large-cap volatility had an inverted (call-over-put) skew while index skew stayed traditional, pushing implied correlation and call-side dispersion to their theoretical minimum before correlation popped.
data_point🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highimplied correlationdispersion tradinglarge caps
So what: Crushed implied correlation is a loaded spring; dispersion books are crowded and vulnerable to unwind.
“implied call side dispersion is at its theoretical minimum” ▶ 7:40
Dispersion and other VIX-tracking strategies can only exit by buying back index volatility, so correlation and index vol now trade nearly one-for-one, making long VIX futures or VIX calls one of the simplest hedges; he thinks that trade has not fully played out and would not be surprised by a VIX spike back to 40-50.
causal🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highVIXVIX futuresdispersion trading
So what: Consider VIX futures/calls as a correlation-unwind hedge; a 40 VIX implies roughly 2.5-3% daily S&P moves.
“one of the simplest hedges today one could do is think about uh buying for instance you know the VIX futures or call options on the VIX” ▶ 8:16
Cboe's single-stock VIX EQ was at 50 against a VIX of 30, versus VIX EQ 50 and VIX 17 in July, showing index vol rising sharply without commensurate movement in single-stock vol, i.e. a correlation-driven move.
data_point🗣 Dean Curnutt (host)conviction: highVIX EQVIXCboeNvidiaMicrosoft
So what: Track the VIX EQ / VIX spread as a real-time implied-correlation gauge.
E-mini S&P futures depth is the ultimate lightning rod for hidden fragility; liquidity evaporated until the close even on the Anthropic IPO day because market makers stopped making markets.
data_point🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highE-mini S&P futuresAnthropicmarket makers
So what: Monitor E-mini order-book depth rather than implied vol as the true risk signal.
“It's this fragility that's hidden.” ▶ 10:04
Too many strategies (dispersion, risk parity, trend following) key off the same volatility number, so every 5-7 years they de-risk simultaneously and discover the market's depth cannot absorb it; liquidity when not needed is mistaken for liquidity when needed.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highrisk paritytrend followingdispersionVIX
So what: Expect periodic cascades when a common risk metric triggers; position for liquidity gaps rather than trusting quoted depth.
“People keep misinterpreting the liquidity when you don't need it for the liquidity available when you do need it.” ▶ 10:59
Pod-shop crowding as an embedded call option 1
Multi-manager platforms effectively hold a portfolio of call options on their pods: tight stop-losses (e.g. out at -3%) plus large upside payouts incentivize each pod to run maximum leverage in the sponsored, working trade and hope to exit first, creating crowded, simultaneous exits.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highpod shopsmulti-manager hedge funds
So what: Pod deleveraging creates market-level cascades; watch prime-broker de-grossing data as a stress signal.
“Everything is a call option. If it goes down 3%, you're out.” ▶ 12:57
Bimodality: why far-OTM calls on AI megacaps trade at 80-100% vol 4
Long-dated implied vol on Micron and similar AI stocks traded at 80-100%, so a 150%-out-of-the-money option could carry a Black-Scholes delta above 0.5, which is nonsensical if delta approximates the probability of finishing in the money.
data_point🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highMicronBlack-Scholes
So what: Extreme long-dated vol signals the market is not pricing a unimodal distribution; standard Greeks mislead.
“So you can have a 150% out of the money option with a delta over.5” ▶ 0:34
In late June one could do a zero-cost two-year collar on Micron (then around a trillion-dollar company) buying a 10% out-of-the-money put and selling a 170% out-of-the-money call, showing how distorted vol and skew had become.
data_point🗣 Dean Curnutt (host)conviction: highMicron
So what: Such inverted skew lets holders hedge downside cheaply by selling richly priced upside.
One supply/demand driver of rich long-dated vol is levered ETFs: their market makers hedge by buying ratchet options that reset at-the-money strikes monthly, lifting the whole vol term structure.
causal🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: mediumlevered ETFsratchet optionsmarket makers
So what: Levered-ETF hedging flows are a persistent bid for single-stock vol in AI names.
Bimodality hypothesis: if a stock has two disparate outcomes (AI wins and chips trade like a scarce commodity vs. a 2000-style bust), total variance is driven by the squared difference of the expected returns, so 80-90% implied vol is rational even when each scenario carries only 20-30% vol.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highMicronAIsemiconductors
So what: In a zone of maximum confusion, options act as binary prediction markets; don't fade high vol purely on unimodal reasoning.
“your variance is actually a function of the difference in the expected return squared” ▶ 22:12
Anthropic IPO volatility surface and the stock-as-commodity question 4
Anthropic IPO'd at about $1.6 trillion six years after founding, debuting on a 2% down day for the S&P with roughly $230 billion in the bank; listed options were expected to start trading the following Monday and become liquid fast, as SpaceX's did after its ~$1.5 trillion IPO.
data_point🗣 Dean Curnutt (host)conviction: highAnthropicSpaceXS&P 500
So what: Watch the first Anthropic option prints as a live read on AI-ecosystem sentiment.
Bhansali expects Anthropic implied vol to be extremely high, like long-dated Micron and SpaceX, with forced buyers of both tails and little selling: as with locked-up SpaceX holders, puts are prohibitively expensive (~30% premium for one year at ~60% vol) and selling American-style calls to fund puts risks early exercise in a squeeze without deliverable stock, so a Mark Cuban/Yahoo-style collar is impractical with tiny float.
prediction🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: mediumAnthropicMicronSpaceXMark CubanYahoo
So what: Vol sellers face squeeze risk; buyers are forced; expect a rich surface on both wings.
Curnutt forecasts Anthropic's vol surface will have an inverted (downward-sloping) term structure, as SpaceX's did when realized vol fell from about 100 to 50, plus more put skew than SpaceX because price will be a key barometer of frontier-lab health given opaque pre-IPO metrics.
prediction🗣 Dean Curnutt (host)conviction: mediumAnthropicSpaceXfrontier labs
So what: Anthropic put skew may become a tradable gauge of AI-ecosystem stress.
The key open question is whether low-float mega-listings should be valued like scarce commodities (vol could go to 200) or like financial assets that can be issued in unlimited supply (vol should collapse over years, favoring vol sellers); heavy AI capex may force large future stock issuance.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: exploratoryAnthropicMicronSpaceXcommoditiesAI capex
So what: The answer determines whether long-dated AI vol is a sale or a buy; dilution from capex funding favors the financial-asset view.
“commodity traders probably should be trading, you know, ENT uh stock options more than us guys” ▶ 33:56
Path to a correlation spike: lessons from GFC credit tranches 3
Bhansali maps the current equity correlation trade to GFC synthetic credit: super-senior CDX tranche protection was seen as default-proof, but forced exits as spreads widened drove tranche implied correlation from very low to very high, which he profited from buying protection at PIMCO.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highCDXsynthetic creditPIMCOGFC
So what: Implied correlation can spike from positioning unwinds before realized correlation moves.
He expects the first leg up in equity correlation to come from mark-to-market unwinds of dispersion and other correlation-based trades as the perceived risk of an economywide AI-trade unwinding grows, after which realized correlation follows, cascading.
prediction🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: mediumdispersionAI tradeimplied correlation
So what: Position for implied correlation to lead realized; the proximate catalyst is an AI-trade unwind.
The endgame comes when a liquidity-rich participant steps in to sell extreme implied correlation to forced sellers; maintaining liquidity to buy when smart money is running over each other is the core edge, an arc Bhansali has seen at least five times.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highliquidityimplied correlation
So what: Keep dry powder to be the liquidity provider at peak dislocation.
“You maintain liquidity. You maintain potency.” ▶ 38:57
Fed hiking into turmoil and the asymmetric 2-year Treasury 4
Per Bloomberg, the Fed has hiked only once with the VIX above 30 the day before (June 2022, VIX 32.60, policy rate 87bp, inflation 8%), framing the December 9 meeting as a test of whether the Fed hikes into turmoil.
data_point🗣 Dean Curnutt (host)conviction: mediumFederal ReserveVIXBloomberg
So what: A hike with VIX above 30 would be historically rare; watch for a dovish turn.
Bhansali says the Fed's financial-stability 'third mandate' is very much alive and the likelihood of a pivot after a big crash is very high, though it would be signaled via language about watching financial conditions and Treasury-market liquidity rather than an explicit no-hike pledge.
prediction🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: highFederal ReserveWorshTreasury market
So what: Listen for 'financial conditions' and 'Treasury liquidity' language as the pivot code.
“we are watching financial conditions at this point” ▶ 42:16
The 2-year Treasury at about 4.75% is asymmetric: a 300bp rise roughly breaks even over one year thanks to carry and roll-down against ~1.8 duration, while a 300bp fall in a crisis pivot returns roughly 10-15%.
thesis🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: high2-year TreasuryFederal Reserve
So what: Favor front-end Treasuries over long duration (inflation still a problem) as a convex crisis hedge.
“That's a pretty darn asymmetric trade right now.” ▶ 43:41
Bhansali's portfolio design principle for bimodal uncertainty is yield plus optionality: hold carry such as 2-year Treasuries and spend a slice of the yield on convex options in either direction, rather than cute single-stock or forward-vol trades.
framework🗣 Vineer Bhansali (LongTail Alpha)conviction: high2-year Treasuryoptions
So what: Fund tail convexity from safe carry; a simple, robust structure for an uncertain regime.
“yield plus optionality is the framework” ▶ 45:59

Why Is Everything Crashing?55 min26 insights2026-11-21
▶ Watch on YouTube
Jordi Visser argues AI is 'destroying capitalism' by making moats and long-term cash flows unknowable, driving structural multiple compression and AI-linked cyber fears behind the sell-off (Nvidia ~40% off highs) despite intact fundamentals and a successful Anthropic IPO. He frames Bitcoin as the purest AI trade with tokenization as the long-run bridge, but stays out until Bitcoin and major indices reclaim their 200-day averages, watching for a breadth thrust, Fed softening and a year-end pain-trade rally.

AI uncertainty breaking long-term capitalism and institutional investing 5
Jordi Visser says institutional money managers are the most beaten-up and despondent he has ever seen, after a huge deleveraging phase with funds facing closure and redemptions, even though fundamentals and the economy have not changed.

Visser argues AI is destroying capitalism because model release speed makes competition for any idea infinite, so no business can build a durable moat; the future looks like serial entrepreneurship and revenue streams rather than long-lived companies.
thesis🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highAIOpus 5.5AstraGemini
So what: Discount durable-moat assumptions for software/app businesses; favor firms with non-AI barriers.

contrarian🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highS&P 500multiple compression

ETF wrappers unlocking asset classes: GLD to Bitcoin ETFs 4

anecdote🗣 Will Rindconviction: highGLDWorld Gold Councilgold

framework🗣 Will Rindconviction: highGLDBitcoin ETFsETF
So what: Look for asset classes not yet in a 'marquee ETF' wrapper as candidates for demand step-changes.

causal🗣 Anthony Pompliano / Will Rindconviction: highBitcoin ETFsGLDBlackRock

framework🗣 Lance Robertsconviction: mediuminvestor psychology

data_point🗣 Lance Robertsconviction: mediumS&P 500

framework🗣 Lance Robertsconviction: mediummarket pricing

The S&P 500 broke down out of a long sideways consolidation range and is now more than three standard deviations below its 50-day moving average, with MACD as oversold as at the March-April lows of the Iran crisis.
data_point🗣 Lance Robertsconviction: highS&P 500MACD50-day moving averageIran crisis

Roberts' study of 200-day moving average breaks finds that if the market stays below the 200-DMA for fewer than 20 trading days it has historically been a buying opportunity, while more than 20 trading sessions below generally signals a larger correction.
framework🗣 Lance Robertsconviction: high200-day moving average
So what: Mark mid-December as the decision point: a recapture of the 200-DMA before then favors the bullish path.

Venture capitalist and SpaceX board member Steve Jurvetson joins the Moonshots panel to dissect the Anthropic IPO (and why Wall Street cannot digest mega-listings), the frontier-lab race (Anthropic first, Google rising on TPUs and release velocity, OpenAI lagging), and SpaceX's emergence as a low-cost compute hyperscaler with $120.5B of Anthropic agreements. Mega-IPOs: Anthropic and SpaceX test public-market plumbing 6
Anthropic's IPO raised about $81 billion, popped 17% on day one, fell 12% the next day and traded down to its offering price, which Jurvetson called a bad sign because stabilization agents then mask true demand.
data_point🗣 Steve Jurvetsonconviction: highAnthropic
So what: Expect choppy, underwriter-supported trading in mega-IPOs; offering price is a soft floor, not a signal of fair value.
“Actually, it hit its offering price uh in trading just an hour ago, which is really bad.” ▶ 12:41
Jurvetson argues the Anthropic IPO follows the Meta/Facebook IPO pattern: Wall Street's distribution machinery cannot digest that much stock in a short window, so early post-IPO weakness reflects indigestion rather than fundamentals.
causal🗣 Steve Jurvetsonconviction: highAnthropicMetaFacebookMorgan StanleySpaceX
So what: Post-IPO dips in mega-listings (Anthropic, SpaceX) may be buying opportunities as mega-funds slowly build positions.
“I really feel like the machinery is not really geared up for this scale yet.” ▶ 13:49
Large institutions such as Fidelity typically take two to three weeks to evaluate a breakthrough and then accumulate positions over roughly three months, so even major news (e.g., a solved Millennium Prize problem) cannot reprice a mega-cap AI stock overnight.
framework🗣 Steve Jurvetsonconviction: mediumFidelityNewbergerAnthropic
So what: Fundamental breakthroughs at AI mega-caps may be priced in with a multi-week lag, creating drift opportunities.
“you can't have that kind of breakthrough move a stock to where it probably should be overnight at this scale” ▶ 15:48
The Anthropic and SpaceX IPOs were of similar size and individually and combined dwarfed prior records, together roughly 3x the next biggest IPO, Saudi Aramco, motivated by the capital needed for AI infrastructure.
data_point🗣 Steve Jurvetsonconviction: highAnthropicSpaceXSaudi Aramco
So what: Public markets are becoming the funding channel for AI capex; IPO reception now directly governs how much compute frontier labs can buy.
Because AI model investment has no natural budget cap (unlike Apollo), whether IPOs are over- or under-subscribed now determines Anthropic's GPU spend and therefore the size of the next neural net; Wall Street demand effectively sets the pace of AI progress.
causal🗣 Steve Jurvetsonconviction: highAnthropicApollo programGPUs
So what: Watch IPO/follow-on demand for AI labs as a leading indicator of GPU orders.
“AI has infinite scalability in terms of the amount you could invest in the size of the next neural net.” ▶ 29:53

contrarian🗣 Panelist (co-host)conviction: mediumAnthropic

“how do you optimize for a 50-year mission when you're evaluating being evaluated every quarter on your results?” ▶ 32:10

Jurvetson believes we are in a period of maximal vulnerability of institutional infrastructure because AI can analyze legacy human-written code and even patches to find exploits, but systems hardened by armies of AI testers should improve over time.

Jurvetson's investing playbook and mission-driven companies 5
DFJ's partnership initially voted down the 2008 SpaceX investment; two partners flipped only after adding structure (anti-dilution, a 2x minimum IPO conversion, 2x liquidation preference and a 2x redemption after ~5 years) because they could not see SpaceX worth more than 2-3x entry based on ULA, Boeing and Lockheed comparables.
anecdote🗣 Steve Jurvetsonconviction: highSpaceXDFJUnited Launch AllianceBoeingLockheed

framework🗣 Steve Jurvetsonconviction: highHotmail

CME vs prediction markets and perpetuals 9
Terry Duffy argues perpetual futures are legally swaps, not futures, because the funding rate is two parties exchanging payments, and expects CME to prevail in court.
thesis🗣 Terry Duffy (CME Group CEO)conviction: highCME Groupperpetual futuresCFTC
So what: A swap ruling would upend perps venues' registration and tax status; regulatory risk for crypto/retail derivative platforms.
“the definition of a swap is two parties exchanging a payment to each other. And that's what that's how the funding rate works for perpetuals.” ▶ 31:00
If perpetuals are deemed swaps, Duffy warns retail traders could lose Section 1256 tax treatment (60% long-term/40% short-term) and face ordinary income taxes, plus platforms would be out of compliance on swap registration.
causal🗣 Terry Duffy (CME Group CEO)conviction: highperpetual futures

Crypto capitulation: Bitcoin loses the 200-week MA 9
Bitcoin fell about 12.5% over seven days and sits roughly 8% below its 200-week moving average (~68K), matching the depth below that average seen at the mid-2025 ~56K bottom.
data_point🗣 David (co-host)conviction: highBitcoin
So what: Distance below the 200-week MA is the key regime gauge; watch whether it widens toward FTX-like levels.
“Bitcoin is 8% below its 200 week, which was which is currently as much down below its 200 week as it was during the 56,000 bottom” ▶ 5:37
Bitcoin has printed seven consecutive red weekly candles, something that has happened only two or three times before in its history, in what looks like a liquidation cascade.
data_point🗣 David (co-host)conviction: highBitcoin
So what: Rare streaks like this tend to cluster near capitulation; useful as a contrarian timing input.
“Bitcoin's at seven weeks, seven red uh weekly candles in a row, which has only happened like two or three times before in Bitcoin's history.” ▶ 6:31
Michael Nato calls this the third capitulation since the 10/10 crash (after February and June); key levels are the 50-week MA at 75K (lost), the 200-week MA at 68K (lost), the June low at 58K (holding), and a 'basement' at the on-chain realized price of about 53K.
framework🗣 Ryan (co-host)conviction: mediumMichael NatoBitcoinThe DeFi Report
So what: Map of support levels: 58K June low then ~53K realized price are the levels to watch for further downside or bottom-buying.
If Bitcoin falls to the ~53K realized price it would be about 22% below its 200-week MA, which is FTX-level territory without an FTX-type event, and would signal a structural break of Bitcoin's long-term trend.
thesis🗣 David (co-host)conviction: mediumBitcoinFTX
So what: A break below 58K/53K would invalidate long-term trend models; treat it as a regime-change signal rather than a dip.

Six narratives for the sell-off 7
One explanation for the crypto sell-off is macro tightening: CPI keeps printing hot, the dollar is rising, and talk of renewed Fed hiking echoes Michael Howell's call that a liquidity-shrinking 'turbulence era' favors bonds over risk assets.
causal🗣 Ryan (co-host)conviction: mediumFederal ReserveCPIMichael HowellUS dollar
So what: If liquidity is the driver, watch the dollar, CPI and Fed pricing rather than crypto-native catalysts.
A second explanation is the AI trade unwinding: Nasdaq/QQQ is down about 13-14% back to April levels and Nvidia's sharp drop may have triggered margin calls in Asia and reflexive capital flight from risk assets including crypto.
causal🗣 Ryan (co-host)conviction: mediumNvidiaQQQNASDAQAI
So what: Crypto is trading as a high-beta AI-risk proxy; Nvidia is a key cross-asset tell.
A third explanation is retail selling crypto to fund purchases of the Anthropic IPO, with analysts pointing to a ~30% Bitcoin dump right before the SpaceX IPO as precedent.
causal🗣 Ryan (co-host)conviction: lowAnthropicSpaceXBitcoin

Other narratives include a hidden seller or blown-out margin linked to the earlier Binance hack (CZ denies Binance is selling) and general chaos such as bank outages, FAA groundings, cyberattacks and 'normie' prepping.

causal🗣 Ryan (co-host)conviction: mediumMichael NatoBitcoin minersBitcoin

Exchange convergence, AI trading, and regulatory clashes 4
Coinbase now lets US users trade crypto, stocks and futures directly from ChatGPT, and Ryan expects every brokerage, including Robinhood, to follow as AI agents become a personal CIO from strategy to execution.
prediction🗣 Ryan (co-host)conviction: mediumCoinbaseChatGPTRobinhood

The CFTC granted Backpack DCM and DCO licenses, making it the first exchange with blockchain-based order execution to join the short list of CFTC-licensed venues like Coinbase, Kraken and Kalshi.

Variational raised a Series B at a $1.5 billion valuation as an RFQ-style brokerage/aggregator rather than an order-book exchange, part of a convergence of perps, prediction markets and exchanges like Hyperliquid and Lighter into super apps.

So what: Exchange categories are collapsing into super apps; differentiation shifts to execution and distribution.
Kalshi's co-founders were summoned to a Utah criminal court on 13 felony counts tied to state gambling law even as federal regulators and the CFTC approve of Kalshi, highlighting a states-versus-federal clash over prediction markets.
data_point🗣 Ryan (co-host)conviction: highKalshiCFTCUtah
So what: State-level legal risk is a live overhang for prediction-market businesses despite federal clearance.

So what: Solana is acting like a company buying distribution; signals a corporate-style growth strategy.
