Wealthion 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

I Thought Oil Would Go to the Moon. I Was Wrong | Art Berman53 min25 insights2026-11-20
▶ Watch on YouTube
Geologist Art Berman argues supply-demand balance has zero predictive power for oil prices and that comparative inventory (deviation from the five-year seasonal average) is the signal that matters; inventories are in deficit but far less than in 2022, explaining why the Iran war and Strait of Hormuz disruption did not send oil to $200. He sees higher-for-longer prices driven by a ~$35/bbl logistics premium until demand destruction, with China possibly already the poster child, and suggests integrated oil majors as a place to start.
Supply/demand models fail; comparative inventory predicts oil price · 8Why oil didn't hit $200: system adaptability and buffers · 7China demand destruction · 4Higher for longer, then demand destruction · 3Refining capital and integrated oil majors · 3
Supply/demand models fail; comparative inventory predicts oil price 8
Art Berman argues that the oil supply-demand balance, the main approach most analysts use, has had zero correlation with oil price over the last 25 years.
contrarian🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highoilsupply-demand balance
So what: Discount sell-side oil forecasts built primarily on supply/demand balance models.
“Supply demand balance, which is probably the the main approach that most analysts use, has zero correlation to price over the last 25 years.” ▶ 4:59
Berman says supply and demand each correlate with oil price but do not predict it; they appear to work only in extreme demand shocks such as COVID in 2020 and the 2008 financial crisis, and failed in surrounding years and during the Ukraine war.
framework🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highCOVID2008 financial crisisUkraine war
So what: Supply/demand signals are only reliable in demand-collapse regimes; don't extrapolate them to normal markets.
“because it works sometimes and it makes sense that it should work, continue to pretend that it works.” ▶ 8:14
Berman's 25-year comparative inventory method subtracts the five-year seasonal average inventory for the same date from current inventory; a negative value is a deficit and a positive value a surplus, and markets price off this seasonal comparison rather than absolute barrels.
framework🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highcomparative inventoryWTIOECD
So what: Track US weekly DOE and OECD monthly inventories versus their five-year seasonal average as the core oil price signal.
“Markets care about how things are seasonally.” ▶ 12:16
Berman claims comparative inventory works about 80% of the time as a price indicator, versus about 1% for supply and demand.
data_point🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highcomparative inventory
So what: Gives a quantitative basis for favoring inventory-deviation models over balance models.
“it works about 80% of the time compared with supply and demand which works about 1% of the time.” ▶ 12:55
Inventory data quality varies: the US Department of Energy reports weekly, the IEA and EIA give monthly OECD numbers, while China's inventories are estimated, partly via satellite imagery.
data_point🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumUS Department of EnergyIEAEIAOECDChina
So what: Weight US/OECD inventory signals more heavily; treat China stock estimates with uncertainty.
Berman says current comparative oil inventories are in deficit to the five-year average but far less so than in 2022 during the Ukraine war, which explains why oil prices are not as high as they were then.
causal🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highoil inventoriesUkraine war
So what: Prices should not be expected to reach 2022 peaks unless the inventory deficit deepens to 2022 levels.
Berman attributes the roughly $20 rise in oil since early August (when WTI was about $83 and Brent about $88) to comparative inventory moving further toward deficit, plus markets accepting that the US-Iran conflict will not be resolved soon.
causal🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highWTIBrentIranUnited States
So what: Watch the direction of inventory deficit and the odds of a US-Iran deal as the two main drivers of the next leg in crude.
“Because comparative inventory is marching farther towards deficit.” ▶ 14:24
Berman says the prospects of refilling oil inventories against the current geopolitical backdrop of a prolonged Iran war and Houthi attacks on Saudi Arabia are essentially zero.
prediction🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highIranHouthisSaudi Arabia
So what: Inventory deficits are likely to persist, supporting a structurally elevated oil price while the conflict lasts.
Why oil didn't hit $200: system adaptability and buffers 7
Berman admits that at the start of the Iran war he, like many, expected oil to 'go to the moon' because about 20 million barrels a day of oil and gas equivalent (about 20% of world supply) passes through the Strait of Hormuz, but he was wrong because he ignored the system's adaptability.
anecdote🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highStrait of HormuzIran
So what: Chokepoint-closure scenarios overstate price spikes; the system reroutes and buffers.
“Most of us who thought that were wrong, including me.” ▶ 41:55
Berman concludes that oil markets care less about strategic petroleum reserve drawdowns than humans do; despite the OECD drawing roughly 400 million barrels of collective strategic reserves and concerns about the US SPR nearing 'tank bottom', prices have not gone to $200.
contrarian🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumstrategic petroleum reserveSPROECDUnited States
So what: Don't trade SPR drawdown headlines as a direct bullish oil catalyst; commercial comparative inventories matter more.
“the system doesn't care about strategic petroleum reserves as much as humans do.” ▶ 18:28
Berman estimates that oil flows out of the Persian Gulf are around 14 million barrels a day, about 80% of pre-war levels, achieved via tanker-to-tanker transfers, more tankers, and longer routes.
data_point🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumPersian Gulfoil tankers
So what: Gulf export volumes have largely recovered; the constraint is cost and logistics rather than volume.
Berman estimates that the cost of adapting oil logistics during the war (more tankers, longer routes, redundancy, tanker attacks) has added roughly a $35 per barrel premium to oil prices, which accounts for most of the price rise since August.
data_point🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumoil tankersoil
So what: The war premium is a logistics-cost premium; it would compress if routing normalizes, independent of supply/demand.
“All of our ingenuity hasn't been for free.” ▶ 44:09
Berman notes US crude oil and condensate production has reached record levels near 14 million barrels a day, as higher prices act as a reinforcing loop incentivizing producers to drill more and produce marginal oil.
data_point🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highUnited Statescrude oilcondensate
So what: Higher prices are quietly unlocking US supply, a source of medium-term price relief often overlooked.
Berman argues tanker owners will not sit idle inside the Persian Gulf for months; rising oil prices increase their incentive to take the risk of running cargoes out, since otherwise they go out of business.
causal🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumoil tankersPersian Gulf
So what: Expect continued creeping Gulf export recovery as prices rise, capping upside.
Berman says reports of diesel at $200 a barrel in Singapore reflect local logistics-driven scarcity, not the 'true' oil price, and that futures prices are valid because they serve a different purpose such as hedging.
contrarian🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumdieselSingaporefutures markets
So what: Don't read regional product spikes as signals that crude benchmarks are mispriced.
“That's that's a local scarcity problem.”
China demand destruction 4
Berman argues the most probable explanation for falling Chinese oil imports is weak Chinese demand, citing Chinese refinery runs still about 8% lower year over year despite a slight monthly uptick, rather than political deals, SPR use, or green energy.
contrarian🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highChinarefinery runs
So what: Chinese demand weakness is a bearish oil factor the market may be underpricing.
“They're just using less oil.” ▶ 25:22
Berman calls the idea that EV adoption explains China's lower oil use 'ludicrous' as a full explanation: China consumes about 16 million barrels a day and he would be shocked if even 1 million barrels a day had been substituted by EVs.
data_point🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumChinaEVs
So what: EV displacement is real but too small to explain China's import drop; look to economic weakness instead.
Berman cautions that China economic-doom forecasts have been consistently wrong for 20 years; China is willing to run up debt to dominate global supply chains, and still grows around 4%+ GDP.
contrarian🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumChina
So what: Avoid outright China-collapse trades; slower Chinese oil demand can coexist with strategic economic strength.
Berman suggests China may be 'the poster child' of demand destruction; if every country cut consumption as much as China has (a few million barrels a day), oil prices would fall sharply.
prediction🗣 Art Bermanconviction: exploratoryChinademand destruction
So what: Monitor demand destruction spreading beyond China as the key signal for an eventual oil price collapse.
“What if China is is the poster child of what demand destruction looks like”
Higher for longer, then demand destruction 3
Berman believes oil is in a 'higher for longer' situation as long as wartime adaptation adds complexity, redundancy, and inefficiency, raising prices regardless of supply-demand balance, until prices get high enough to destroy demand.
prediction🗣 Art Bermanconviction: highoil

“yes, we are in a higher for longer situation until we're not.” ▶ 46:46
Berman disagrees with Rory Johnston and Javier Blas that a glut will follow the end of the war, noting a glut was also forecast just before the war based on supply-demand analysis he considers unreliable.
contrarian🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumRory JohnstonJavier Blas
So what: Be skeptical of post-war glut forecasts derived from balance models.
Berman notes that after the oil shocks of the 1970s and early 1980s came 15 years of very low oil prices and a poor global economy, and says this cycle may 'rhyme' with that because consumers ultimately cut usage.
framework🗣 Art Bermanconviction: exploratory1970s oil shocks
So what: A prolonged price spike raises the risk of a long subsequent low-price, weak-growth regime.
“But it may rhyme because we're talking about human behavior and people are cheap.” ▶ 49:52
Refining capital and integrated oil majors 3
Berman argues the lack of new US refineries in about 30 years is not underinvestment but capital markets rationally declining multi-decade, multibillion-dollar bets amid 20 years of peak-demand and energy-transition narratives.

Berman notes new refineries are being built mainly with state capital in Nigeria, the Middle East, and China, rather than by investor-driven companies.
data_point🗣 Art Bermanconviction: mediumNigeriaMiddle EastChinarefineries

Odd Lots · Bloomberg 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

How AI Is Reshaping China's Industrial Policy | Odd Lots50 min25 insights2026-11-21
▶ Watch on YouTube
Brookings fellow Kyle Chan argues Beijing prioritizes high-tech self-reliance over GDP and consumption, using industrial policy and export-control pressure to push Chinese AI labs onto Huawei Ascend chips despite production bottlenecks in SMIC capacity, HBM and lithography. The trade-off is a risk of falling behind the US AI frontier, while low barriers to entry and overcapacity explain why Chinese stocks rarely reward investors.
Beijing's goal: tech self-reliance over GDP and consumption · 4Full-stack conglomerates and cross-sector spillovers · 4Huawei Ascend: from inference to training on domestic chips · 6Production bottlenecks: SMIC capacity, HBM and lithography · 6Self-reliance vs. staying at the AI frontier · 2Why Chinese equities don't pay investors · 3
Beijing's goal: tech self-reliance over GDP and consumption 4
Kyle Chan argues Xi Jinping prioritizes high-tech self-reliance and winning 'industries of the future' over GDP growth, consumers or households, so consumer stimulus and bailouts are treated as secondary to the tech push.
contrarian🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highXi JinpingChina
So what: Don't expect large consumer-demand stimulus from Beijing; position for continued supply-side/industrial support rather than a consumption rebound.
“if you start with the premise that the goal is not GDP growth, in and of itself, the goal is high tech self-reliance of sort of like winning the industries of the future” ▶ 0:45
Beijing's operating theory ('new quality productive forces' plus a 'complete industrial system') is that winning high-tech sectors with full domestic supply chains will eventually deliver growth and 'purple collar' technical jobs such as maintaining robots.
framework🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: mediumnew quality productive forcesrobotics
So what: Policy keywords like 'new quality productive forces' signal where state capital and local-government support will flow.
China's five-year plan explicitly lists priority sectors — chips, AI, robotics, quantum, fusion, biotech, brain-computer interfaces — and being on that list unlocks local government support and state-backed investment funds.
framework🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highfive year plansemiconductorsAIroboticsquantumfusionbiotechbrain computer interface
So what: Track the five-year plan's priority list as a leading indicator of which Chinese sectors get subsidized capital (and subsequent overcapacity).
Chan says Xi is 'AI-pilled' but Beijing does not see AI as the last technology that will make biotech or other sectors obsolete; instead it wants AI diffused into drug discovery, manufacturing and physical AI/robotics, while keeping biotech and others as 'future pillar industries'.
thesis🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: mediumXi JinpingAIbiotechroboticsphysical AI
So what: China's AI strategy is diffusion/application-focused, favoring industrial AI and robotics adopters rather than a pure frontier-model bet.
Full-stack conglomerates and cross-sector spillovers 4
Chinese tech firms sit at the intersection of many sectors (EVs, batteries, robotics, smartphones, chips, AI): BYD started as a battery maker, which helped make it a powerful EV company, and it now makes its own chips and is moving into autonomous driving and robotics.
data_point🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highBYDEVsbatteriesroboticsautonomous driving
So what: Value Chinese industrials on cross-sector optionality; battery/EV supply-chain depth seeds entry into robotics and chips.
Xiaomi's jump from cheap smartphones and appliances into well-regarded EVs drew on a pre-existing Chinese supply chain and talent pool, whereas Apple's car effort in the 2010s fizzled.
contrarian🗣 Kyle Chan / Joe Weisenthalconviction: mediumXiaomiAppleEVs
So what: China's dense supply-chain ecosystem lowers barriers for new entrants into adjacent hardware categories — a structural disadvantage for US hardware diversification.
What differentiates China from US big tech's full-stack push (Google TPUs/Waymo, Amazon Trainium) is the sheer number of firms doing it — even food-delivery companies build foundation models — and that Chinese firms control the factories and components rather than outsourcing manufacturing as Meta does with VR goggles.
framework🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highGoogleAlibabaAmazonTrainiumMetaXiaomiTPU
So what: Chinese competitors have hardware manufacturing control US platforms lack, enabling faster iteration and lower costs in physical products.
ByteDance worked with ZTE on an agentic smartphone that could operate apps like a human, but major Chinese platforms such as Tencent blocked it to preserve control — a preview of walled-garden fights over agentic commerce.
anecdote🗣 Joe Weisenthal / Kyle Chanconviction: mediumByteDanceZTETencentAI agents
So what: Platform owners will resist third-party AI agents; agentic-commerce economics will be contested globally, not just in China.
“no one wants to just be a passive door” ▶ 17:46
Huawei Ascend: from inference to training on domestic chips 6
Huawei's Ascend AI chips long suffered a chicken-and-egg problem versus Nvidia's CUDA ecosystem — no optimized libraries meant no users and vice versa — but US export controls and a Beijing push are now forcing Chinese AI labs to co-design hardware with Huawei.
causal🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highHuaweiAscendNvidiaCUDA
So what: Export controls are eroding CUDA lock-in inside China, building a domestic software ecosystem that reduces Nvidia's long-run China addressable market.
DeepSeek and Huawei have a tight partnership with personnel embedded at each other's companies, and other Chinese AI labs now announce day-one support for Huawei and other domestic chips.
data_point🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highDeepSeekHuawei
So what: Day-one domestic-chip support is a measurable signal of decoupling progress worth tracking with each Chinese model release.
Huawei chips have been good enough for inference but not for training 2–3 trillion parameter models; Huawei compensates for weaker individual chips by using its networking expertise to bundle many into 'super pods', trading quality for quantity.
data_point🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highHuaweisuper podsAI training
So what: Watch Huawei super-pod deployments as the key metric for whether China can train frontier models domestically.
There are rumors that the next DeepSeek model will be fully pre-trained on Huawei chips, which Chan sees as a potential tipping point.
prediction🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: mediumDeepSeekHuawei
So what: A DeepSeek model fully pre-trained on Huawei hardware would be a negative signal for Nvidia China demand and a positive one for Chinese chip suppliers.
“there's rumors that the next deep sea model, for example, will be fully pre-trained on Huawei chips” ▶ 21:47
Chinese AI labs (DeepSeek, Alibaba and others) would prefer to keep using Nvidia and were eager to order the H200s Trump allowed to be exported, but Beijing is pushing back, wanting them to partner with domestic chipmakers instead.
thesis🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highNvidiaH200DeepSeekAlibabaBeijingTrump
So what: US export approvals don't guarantee Nvidia China sales; Beijing's discouragement caps upside from H200 exports.
Chinese chipmakers love the US export controls and Beijing's self-reliance push because they've seen an astronomical surge in demand that didn't exist before.
data_point🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highChinese chipmakersHuaweiSMIC
So what: Domestic Chinese chip and equipment suppliers are structural beneficiaries of decoupling.
Production bottlenecks: SMIC capacity, HBM and lithography 6
Huawei is production-constrained: it relies on SMIC, China's TSMC analog, whose capacity is limited by lithography equipment and largely allocated to Huawei; Huawei is also building its own separate network of fabs that is still ramping.
data_point🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highHuaweiSMICTSMC
So what: Chinese AI chip volume, not just capability, is the binding constraint — watch SMIC capacity additions and Huawei fab ramps.
HBM is another bottleneck: Chinese memory makers are trying to move from DRAM into HBM and reach 'good enough' within 2–3 years, lagging SK Hynix, Samsung and Micron; reportedly Huawei tells customers to bring their own HBM.
data_point🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highHBMSK HynixSamsungMicronHuaweiDRAM
So what: Leakage of HBM into China and Chinese HBM progress matter for the Hynix/Samsung/Micron oligopoly and for Huawei's output.
Chan believes US export controls had a massively galvanizing effect: China is now trying to replicate the entire global semiconductor supply chain domestically — equipment, materials, photoresists, etch and deposition — which he says no country has ever done.
thesis🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highChinasemiconductorsexport controlsphotoresists
So what: Expect sustained state funding of Chinese semicap and materials; long-run share risk for foreign equipment vendors in China.
“China is almost trying to replicate the entire global semiconductor supply chain in China, which I don't think any country has ever done before” ▶ 25:28
China had deprioritized lithography expecting continued access, and the first-Trump-administration effective ban on ASML EUV machines was a wake-up call that capped Chinese node ambitions; Huawei is now leading a long-term effort, recruiting engineers including from ASML, with a rumored EUV prototype site in Shenzhen.
causal🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highASMLEUVHuaweiShenzhenlithography
So what: A credible Chinese EUV/immersion tool would be a major shock to ASML's China business and to the export-control regime.
Lithography — especially immersion (still largely from ASML) and EUV — is the key bottleneck to China having 'good enough' chips at sufficient volume; workarounds like Huawei's 'logic folding' advanced packaging and multi-patterning exist but multi-patterning lowers yields.
thesis🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highASMLlithographyHuaweimulti-patterningchip packaging
So what: Monitor Chinese yield data and packaging breakthroughs as proxies for how far workarounds can substitute for EUV.
Each part of the chip stack has hundreds or thousands of suppliers, each a potential choke point — e.g., Trumpf lasers and Zeiss lenses for lithography.
framework🗣 Joe Weisenthalconviction: mediumTrumpfZeisslithography
So what: Niche European optics/laser suppliers are critical choke points with pricing power and geopolitical exposure.
Self-reliance vs. staying at the AI frontier 2
Forcing labs onto homegrown chips risks Chinese models falling 9–12 months behind in a frontier race measured in months; Chan frames this as today's goal of closing the gap with the US versus tomorrow's goal of a fully self-reliant 'good enough' supply chain immune to Washington.
framework🗣 Joe Weisenthal / Kyle Chanconviction: highHuaweiNvidiaTSMCUS AI frontier
So what: The China-US AI gap may widen near-term as a policy cost of decoupling; watch benchmark lags of Chinese models.
A bull case for commoditization of intelligence: most economically productive activity can only absorb so much intelligence, so US closed-source labs may earn big margins only in a smaller frontier market while the rest gets commoditized — supporting China's diffusion-first approach.
contrarian🗣 Joe Weisenthal / Tracy Allowayconviction: exploratoryclosed source labsAI commoditization
So what: Questions the durability of frontier-lab pricing power; favors cheap, widely-diffused AI and the businesses applying it.
“most economically productive things in the economy. Do can only absorb so much intelligence” ▶ 47:56
Why Chinese equities don't pay investors 3
Chan says Chinese stock investors rarely profit because sustainable moats are hard: engineers jump ship, local governments finance new entrants, barriers to entry are low, so industries flood with overcapacity even as they become globally competitive exporters.
thesis🗣 Kyle Chanconviction: highChinese equitiesovercapacitylocal governments
So what: Chinese industrial leadership does not translate to shareholder returns; be skeptical of high valuations without proven durable profits.

After a brutal year for Chinese stocks, Kweichow Moutai is again the largest member of the CSI 300, ahead of CATL, and the Shanghai Composite has been roughly flat since 2000.

War on the Rocks 1 episode(s) · 24 insights

What Ukraine Hath Wrought: Lessons for War, If We're Listening38 min24 insights2026-11-20
▶ Watch on YouTube
War on the Rocks panel (Ryan Evans with Michael Kofman, Dara Massicot and Amos Fox) argues the real lessons of nearly four years of war in Ukraine are not 'drones and AI' but force-structure reorganization, the enduring role of mass, attrition and terrain control, and the limits of precision and air power — lessons they say the US failed to apply before its war with Iran (Operation Epic Fury), leaving Middle East and UK bases exposed to drones.
Drone warfare is a force-structure and manpower problem, not a procurement problem · 4Precision denies terrain but cannot control it — mass and attrition still matter · 6Beware porting late-war lessons to the opening of the next war · 2Air power and base defense: gaps exposed in the Iran war · 6Institutional learning failures and who is learning faster · 3Stand-in forces, allies and legacy armor · 3
Drone warfare is a force-structure and manpower problem, not a procurement problem 4
Panelists argue that Ukraine's real lesson is not 'drones and AI' but a fundamental reorganization of core war-fighting functions: embedding drone support and ISR in units, changing billets, and building a high-low mix, which US forces are largely not discussing.
thesis🗣 Dara Massicot (captioned 'Dar Masico')conviction: highdronesISRarmymarines
So what: Defense spend that buys drones without org/manning changes will underdeliver; watch for unit restructuring and billet changes as the real adoption signal.
“it's just a really fundamental reorganization of core war fighting functions that need to happen” ▶ 1:35
Drone warfare at scale is very manpower intensive: Russia and Ukraine did not simply buy drones but strategically reallocated manpower from maneuver formations, plus logistics and organizational capacity, into new support/fire/ISR formations that later also took on air defense.
causal🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highRussiaUkrainedronesISRair defense
So what: Drone adoption implies demand for training, C2, logistics and enabling tech (networks, sensors), not just airframes; vendors of enabling layers benefit.
“at least right now drone warfare is very manpower intensive” ▶ 15:40
Drones work as a huge force multiplier to traditional land-warfare challenges — extensive offensive and defensive mining, artillery that prevents force concentration, and prepared defenses — so a force of only drones would fail; infantry, armor and artillery remain necessary.
thesis🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highdronesartillerymininginfantryarmor
So what: Expect continued demand for artillery, mines and conventional land systems alongside drones rather than substitution.
“you cannot have just a force of drones” ▶ 24:51
Drones have proven to be one of the principal counters to drones in this war, with drone-on-drone fights resembling World War I aerial battles using interception, nets and guns.
data_point🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highdronesinterception
So what: Counter-UAS via interceptor drones is a growth niche; counter-drone and drone markets are coupled.
“drones have proven at least in this war to be one of the principal counters to drones” ▶ 25:01
Precision denies terrain but cannot control it — mass and attrition still matter 6
Amos Fox's 'precision paradox': precision solves problems but creates many more, driving positional warfare, and the West has replaced campaign planning with kill chains, kill webs and targeting.
framework🗣 Amos Foxconviction: highprecision strikekill chainskill webs
So what: Doctrine fixated on strike packages may underinvest in sustained campaign capacity (munitions depth, ground forces).
“we've replaced the idea of campaigns with kill chains and kill webs and targeting” ▶ 4:04
Mass precision lets Ukraine hold terrain on the defense with an incredibly low density of forces, compensating for manpower shortfalls, but it only denies terrain to the enemy; taking and controlling terrain still requires boots on the ground and offensive operations.
thesis🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highUkrainemass precisiondrones
So what: Drone-heavy defense can stabilize fronts but not reverse territorial losses — relevant to war-duration and ceasefire-line expectations.
“It allows you to deny control to your opponent. You still need boots on the ground.” ▶ 11:18
Russia has physical mass and Ukraine does not; Russia's ability to hold terrain is the key obstacle preventing Ukraine from converting military operations into political outcomes.
thesis🗣 Amos Foxconviction: highRussiaUkraine
So what: Supports a base case of a grinding, attritional war where Ukraine's manpower is the binding constraint.
“Russia has the mass. Ukraine does not have the mass” ▶ 5:38
Attrition is unavoidable: reliance on precision strike pushes adversaries underground, into urban terrain and dispersal, which forces the striker into attritional ground fighting whether it wants it or not; forces must be built and resourced at scale for that.
causal🗣 Amos Foxconviction: highprecision strikeattrition
So what: Favors sustained production capacity (munitions, vehicles, replacement manpower) over exquisite low-volume systems.
“It matters how you're going to have to fight.” ▶ 6:07
Fox argues the US obsession with 'restoring maneuver' is misplaced: once forces converge, war becomes positional and attritional, and land forces on a transparent battlefield are like 'an M&M without a hard candy shell' — the priority is restoring protected mobility.
contrarian🗣 Amos Foxconviction: hightransparent battlefieldarmy
So what: Protection/survivability tech for land forces (active protection, counter-drone, signature management) is the unsolved need.
“land forces essentially are operating like an Eminem without a hard candy shell” ▶ 23:06
Russia over-optimized for small-scale infantry assaults and infiltration, an approach that became increasingly combat-inefficient, and failed to invest in restoring force quality, which will take it a while to recover from.
thesis🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: mediumRussiainfantry
So what: Russian offensive efficiency may remain low; watch for force-quality reconstitution as a signal of renewed offensive capacity.
Beware porting late-war lessons to the opening of the next war 2
Drones were not a defining element during the first year and a half of the Ukraine war; they began as artillery spotters, FPV drones emerged in early 2023, and drones only began to shut down mobility around August–September 2023.
data_point🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highfirst-person view dronesUkraineartillery
So what: Opening phases of future wars (e.g., US vs China) may look very different from the current positional phase; don't extrapolate linearly.
“They begin to shut down mobility around August September of 2023.” ▶ 8:09
Kofman warns that militaries tend to assume the opening phase of the next war will look like the last phase of the last war, which is probably wrong; context (e.g., US vs China in the Pacific) and war phasing differ, and some technologies such as the TB2 in Nagorno-Karabakh prove irrelevant in the next conflict.
framework🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highGornocarbachR2 droneChinaUS
So what: Treat 'proven in Ukraine' marketing with caution when sizing defense-tech TAM for Pacific scenarios.
“I think the opening phase of the next war will look like the last phase of the last war I saw and that's probably not going to be it.” ▶ 8:58
Air power and base defense: gaps exposed in the Iran war 6
Panelists say the US failed to apply Ukraine's lessons before Operation Epic Fury against Iran: commercial imagery showed squadrons of high-value aircraft stacked on Middle East bases with air defenses but basically no drone defense, no dispersal and no hardened C2, and the US was lucky Iran did not strike then.
anecdote🗣 Dara Massicot (captioned 'Dar Masico')conviction: highOperation Epic FuryIranMiddle Eastdrone defensecommercial imagery
So what: Base hardening, dispersal and counter-UAS for US overseas bases is an under-funded need now being prioritized.
“we had no hardened C2” ▶ 17:44
The Air Force is only now saying it needs to prioritize base force protection, four years after Russia and Ukraine began hardening bases with underground structures, dispersal, drone nets and decoys.
data_point🗣 Dara Massicot (captioned 'Dar Masico')conviction: highAir Forcedrone netsdecoys
So what: Expect rising budgets for hardened shelters, drone nets, decoys and base counter-drone systems.
“the Air Force says, okay, like we need to prioritize base force protection four years later” ▶ 18:59
A drone threat against RAF Fairford in the UK reportedly forced US bombers to be lofted and moved because of an inability to detect drones, even though such capability exists in Ukraine.
anecdote🗣 Dara Massicot (captioned 'Dar Masico')conviction: mediumFairfordUKbombersdetect drones
So what: Drone detection at allied air bases is an immediate procurement gap; watch for counter-UAS contracts at European bases.
“we had to loft all the bombers and get them out of there because we didn't have the ability to detect drones” ▶ 33:28
The Iran war exposed US bases as vulnerable to asymmetric capabilities, forcing a distant logistics pathway at some cost to aircraft carriers, and panelists say the US has not been open about how hard it was hit in the Middle East.
thesis🗣 Dara Massicot (captioned 'Dar Masico')conviction: mediumIranMiddle Eastaircraft carrierspower projection
So what: Power-projection risk and base vulnerability may drive spending on hardening, dispersal and long-range logistics.
“we haven't talked about how hard we got hit” ▶ 33:12
Air superiority may deliver localized control yet fail to stop an adversary with scaled mass-precision fire control over ground forces, and air power is not able to conduct air defense against these systems at scale without substantial adaptation.
thesis🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highair powerair superiorityair defense
So what: Ground-based point defense and SHORAD for maneuver forces is under-built because of assumed air superiority — a likely growth area.
Russia is procuring over 120,000 large one-way attack drones this year, which air power alone cannot answer.
data_point🗣 Michael Kofman (captioned 'Mike Kaufman')conviction: highRussiaoneway attack dronesGaron

Internet capital markets thesis and the ecosystem 'order of operations' 5
Lily Liu frames Solana's build-out as a sequence: ship the blockchain, then attract developers and applications that can make money, then bring assets (tokenization/RWAs) on chain, with the end goal being an economy rather than just a network.
framework🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highSolanatokenizationreal world assets
So what: Solana's current phase is asset onboarding; watch RWA/tokenized asset issuance metrics as the KPI of the strategy.
“we're building an economy we're not just building a blockchain” ▶ 1:44
Liu describes a spectrum of internet-native finance from the simplest use case (stablecoins and payments) to the most advanced (high-frequency trading of assets), with RWAs increasingly used as collateral in lending and borrowing markets in between.
framework🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highstablecoinspaymentshigh frequency tradingRWADeFi
So what: RWA-as-collateral in on-chain lending is the middle layer to watch for growth between payments and trading venues.
Liu argues the total addressable market for internet-native finance is ~5.5 billion internet users, versus ~1.5 billion for language-bound consumer internet products (English or Chinese speakers), because everyone uses money regardless of language.
thesis🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highSolanainternet capital markets
So what: Frames the bull case for payments/wallet integrations as a larger TAM than prior consumer internet waves.
“what they have in common is not necessarily a language, but the fact that all of those five and a half billion people use money” ▶ 7:24
Liu claims public blockchains will become home to the largest capital market — the internet capital market — because participants trade off almost everything else to be in the largest, most liquid market.
thesis🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highpublic blockchainsinternet capital markets
So what: Liquidity network effects are the core moat thesis; favor chains with unified liquidity.
“people would trade off practically everything else in order to be a part of the largest market” ▶ 11:19
Liu says institutions that long preferred permissioned private chains are shifting to public blockchains, a shift she believes is still accelerating, because public chains offer net new users, distribution and liquidity beyond geographically bounded legacy infrastructure.
prediction🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highpermissioned blockchainspublic blockchainstokenization
So what: Expect more TradFi tokenization launches on public L1s rather than private ledgers; a negative for private-chain vendors.

Tokenization as a fix for passive-index distribution inefficiency 2

Liu compares blockchain capital markets to Uber: just as mobile phones enabled discovery and clearing of latent rider/driver demand, internet capital markets can match mid- and long-tail investment demand with undiscovered, not low-quality, assets.

Finance-first architecture and Solana's competitive positioning 5
Liu says the key difference between ecosystems is whether a chain is a tech platform superpowered by finance (the Web3 'read, write, own' thesis) or financial infrastructure superpowered by technology, and Solana is finance-first, which drives its architectural choices.
framework🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highSolanaWeb3
So what: Use this lens to compare L1 strategies; finance-first chains should prioritize liquidity and trading UX over general-purpose app features.
Solana chose no sharding and a single mainnet to keep unified liquidity, and Liu says if you build on the SVM it is Solana, unlike ecosystems with many teams building separate execution environments.
thesis🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highSolanaSVMsharding

Liu reinterprets past crypto waves as finance: ICOs were v0.1 on-chain capital formation (which she expects to return), NFTs were markets around JPEGs, and blockchain gaming succeeds only when it adds payment rails and in-game item markets rather than running game back ends on chain.
contrarian🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: mediumICONFTsgamingstablecoins

thesis🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: mediumSolana

AI-era security risk for crypto and financial infrastructure 2
Liu predicts a 'pretty tough next 18 months' of AI-driven security incidents hitting financial and transportation infrastructure, with the blockchain industry front and center.

causal🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highAIsecurity

“It's just a different risk function these days than it was even six months ago.” ▶ 25:03

Liu attributes crypto's poor price action to asset-class allocation, not fundamentals: over the last couple of years risk and innovation capital rotated into AI, and blockchain periodically cycles from top to bottom of the frontier-tech pile.
causal🗣 Lily Liu (President, Solana Foundation)conviction: highAIblockchainBitcoin

RealLifeLore 1 episode(s) · 19 insights

Why You Can't Drive Anywhere In This Part of Alaska32 min19 insights2026-11-21
▶ Watch on YouTube
A geography explainer on why Southeast Alaska's ~70,000 residents, including the state capital Juneau, have no road links to North America: maritime-era settlement, extreme terrain and rainfall, and poor per-capita economics doomed projects like the Juneau Access road and Ketchikan's bridge to nowhere, leaving the region dependent on the Alaska Marine Highway ferries and air, with high living costs. It also traces Anchorage's WWII- and air-cargo-driven rise (90% of the industrialized world within 9.5 hours' flight); investor signal is thin.
Juneau's road isolation and capital mismatch · 5Maritime-era settlement vs. Anchorage's military and air-cargo rise · 3Why panhandle roads fail: terrain, weather and cost · 4Ketchikan's bridge to nowhere and Boring Company pitch · 4Alaska Marine Highway and the panhandle cost premium · 3
Juneau's road isolation and capital mismatch 5
Juneau, Alaska's state capital with about 31,000 residents, has no road connection to Anchorage or anywhere else in North America, making it the largest city on the continent not connected by road.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highJuneauAnchorageAlaska
So what: Government-heavy Juneau economy depends entirely on air and ferry links; any access project or capital-relocation move would re-rate local real estate and services.
“Juno is the largest city on the North American continent today in the 21st century that isn't connected to anywhere else on the continent by road” ▶ 2:55
Greater Anchorage holds roughly 400,000 people (more than half of Alaska's population, about 13 times Juneau's) and roughly half of statewide GDP, yet the capital sits in Juneau.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highAnchorageJuneauAlaska
So what: Alaska's economic center of gravity is Anchorage; state-facing businesses and lobbying face a structural travel cost to reach government.
Driving from Anchorage to Juneau requires about 750 miles through Alaska and Canada's Yukon Territory to Haines plus a 4.5-hour ferry and 2-hour check-in, totaling up to about 25 hours, versus a direct flight under 2 hours.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highAnchorageJuneauHainesYukon TerritoryAlaska Marine Highway System
So what: Air carriers effectively hold pricing power on the Anchorage-Juneau corridor; ferry reliability is a key local risk factor.
Alaska has voted on at least nine capital-relocation ballot measures, more than any other state; voters approved moving the capital to Willow in 1974 with 56.6% but in 1982 53% rejected the $2.8 billion funding, and later Wasilla (1994) and legislature-relocation (2002) measures also failed.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highWillowWasillaJuneauAlaska
So what: Voters back relocation in principle but refuse to fund it; capital-move risk to Juneau's government-driven economy is low near term.
“voters approved the ballot to move the capital, but then later rejected the ballot that would have actually funded it” ▶ 13:36
No legal attempt to move Alaska's capital has been made in nearly a quarter century, and the capital will likely not be moved anytime soon.
prediction🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: mediumJuneauAlaska
So what: Juneau's state-employment base looks durable; supports stable local demand for housing and services.
Maritime-era settlement vs. Anchorage's military and air-cargo rise 3
Southeast Alaska's towns grew around harbors, fisheries, mineral deposits and shipping routes when ships were the main transport, so they were never designed to be road-connected; the 1880 Juneau gold strike and 1896 Klondike rush made Juneau overtake Sitka, and the capital moved there in 1906.
causal🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highJuneauSitkaKlondike gold rushKetchikanPetersburgWrangell
So what: Path dependence: legacy maritime geography still dictates logistics costs and infrastructure needs in the region today.
“these communities emerged when ships, not automobiles, were still the principal means of transportation” ▶ 8:03
Anchorage had only about 3,500 people in 1940 versus nearly 5,700 in Juneau, but WWII military investment (Elmendorf Air Force Base, Fort Richardson) and the 1942 Alaska Highway drove it to over 11,000 by 1950 and over 100,000 by 1970, versus Juneau's roughly 13,000.
causal🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highAnchorageJuneauElmendorf Air Force BaseFort RichardsonAlaska HighwayUS military
So what: Defense spending can create a regional economic center almost from scratch; military basing remains a core driver of Anchorage's economy.
Anchorage became a key air-cargo hub because about 90% of the industrialized world is within 9.5 hours' flight, including Tokyo, Seoul, Beijing, Berlin, Paris, London, New York, Houston and Los Angeles, a role that began with Cold War airspace restrictions on Soviet and Chinese routes.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highAnchorageair cargoTokyoBeijingLondonNew York
So what: Anchorage's airport is a structural chokepoint for transpacific air freight; watch cargo airlines and logistics operators with Anchorage hubs as trade-flow beneficiaries.
“approximately 90% of the industrialized world is reachable within just 9 and a half hours of the city by air” ▶ 10:32
Why panhandle roads fail: terrain, weather and cost 4
Southeast Alaska covers an area slightly larger than Maine with about 70,000 full-time residents (nearly 1 in 10 Alaskans), yet only Haines, Skagway and Hyder have road links, all routed through Canada, and Juneau, Ketchikan and Sitka each have separate unconnected road systems.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highSoutheast AlaskaHainesSkagwayJuneauKetchikanSitkaCanada
So what: A fragmented, captive regional market reliant on ferries and aircraft, favoring incumbents in marine freight and regional aviation.
Southeast Alaska is effectively a flooded mountain range with around 1,000 named islands in the Alexander Archipelago, and Ketchikan receives about 145 inches of precipitation a year versus about 39 in Seattle, raising landslide, washout, flood and avalanche risk that makes roads costly to build and maintain.
causal🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highAlexander ArchipelagoKetchikanSeattleCoast Mountains
So what: Any infrastructure project in the region carries high construction and maintenance cost-overrun risk.
The Juneau Access Improvement Project, a 48-mile highway along Lynn Canal to a new ferry terminal cutting the Haines crossing to about 30 minutes, was approved by the Federal Highway Administration in 2006 but crossed about 40 avalanche zones, faced environmental litigation, ballooned past $570 million, was dropped by the governor in 2016 amid a budget crisis and ended federally in 2018.
anecdote🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highJuneau Access Improvement ProjectFederal Highway AdministrationLynn CanalHainesSkagway
So what: Illustrates how litigation and fiscal stress kill remote infrastructure; discount announced Alaska megaprojects accordingly.
In March 2025 Alaska launched a new feasibility study, the Chilkat Connector, examining a western Lynn Canal route with fewer avalanche zones plus a shorter ferry crossing, though nothing is approved and cost and environmental questions remain.
prediction🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: exploratoryChilkat ConnectorLynn CanalHainesJuneauAlaska
So what: Early-stage project to monitor for engineering/construction contract opportunities, but history suggests a low probability of build.
Ketchikan's bridge to nowhere and Boring Company pitch 4
Ketchikan (about 8,000 residents) is not connected by land to its own airport on Gravina Island, opened in 1973, so all passengers, workers and vehicles take a 5-minute ferry across the Tongass Narrows, which drastically reduces airport efficiency.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highKetchikanKetchikan International AirportGravina IslandTongass Narrows
So what: Persistent demand for a fixed crossing keeps generating infrastructure proposals in the town.
The Gravina Island Bridge ('bridge to nowhere') reached nearly $400 million in estimated cost, drew national criticism as wasteful federal earmark spending, and was abandoned in September 2007, yet Alaska still built a 3.2-mile, roughly $26 million access road that serves no purpose.
anecdote🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highGravina Island BridgeKetchikanGravina Islandfederal earmarks
So what: Case study in stranded infrastructure capex from politically driven earmarks.
“The access highway cost approximately $26 million to build and serves zero purpose whatsoever.” ▶ 21:47
In early 2026 Ketchikan officials submitted a proposal to Elon Musk's Boring Company to build a vehicle tunnel beneath the Tongass Narrows to the airport, though no tunnel has been approved.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: exploratoryBoring CompanyElon MuskKetchikanTongass Narrows
So what: Signal of municipal interest in Boring Company tunneling as a cheaper alternative to bridges; watch whether it advances to a contract.
“Ketchacan officials submitted an interesting proposal to Elon Musk's Boring Company to construct a vehicle tunnel beneath the Tongas Narrows” ▶ 22:12
A continental road link for Ketchikan would need marine crossings to the mainland and a long mountain highway toward British Columbia's Cassiar Highway via routes like Bradfield Canal, and limited potential traffic makes the economics nearly impossible to justify, so Sitka, Petersburg, Wrangell and others will likely stay disconnected for a long time.
prediction🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highKetchikanCassiar HighwayBradfield CanalBritish ColumbiaSitkaPetersburgWrangell
So what: Expect continued reliance on ferries and air rather than new road links; structural isolation persists.
Alaska Marine Highway and the panhandle cost premium 3
The Alaska Marine Highway System, established in 1963, runs publicly operated ferries over about 3,500 miles of coastal routes serving more than 30 communities with links to Canada and the lower 48, carrying passengers, vehicles and freight on trips that can last overnight or several days.
data_point🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highAlaska Marine Highway SystemCanadaJuneauKetchikanSitka
So what: The ferry system is critical, state-funded infrastructure; its budgets and fleet renewal drive shipbuilding and service demand in the region.
“Alaska effectively uses the waterways between the mountains themselves as its highways instead” ▶ 24:12
Ferries are slower, capacity- and schedule-constrained, expensive to run in North Pacific waters, and vulnerable to weather, breakdowns and aging ships, so dependence on ships and aircraft for food, construction materials and fuel makes nearly everything in the panhandle more expensive than road-accessible North America.
causal🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highAlaska Marine Highway SystemSoutheast Alaskafuelconstruction materials
So what: Structurally high cost of living and inputs in Southeast Alaska; supports pricing power for regional shippers, airlines and fuel distributors, and raises costs for local businesses.
Building roads, bridges and tunnels across the panhandle would probably cost billions of dollars to serve only about 70,000 people, which is especially hard to justify at the federal level.
thesis🗣 Narrator (RealLifeLore)conviction: highSoutheast Alaskafederal government
So what: Low ROI per capita means federal infrastructure dollars are unlikely to flow here at scale; maritime and aviation remain the default.
